- the only place we need to update an issue file when the issue is partially complete is the "current behavior" section. We don't need to add append-logs to the end, it's better to update the main body to align with the current design, and the current behavior with the current status.

```

### feedback_cross_language_pitfalls.md

```markdown
---
name: Cross-language design pitfalls
description: Three approaches the user rejected when designing SoraMech's cross-language wire format
type: feedback
originSessionId: e9ed70c7-2b63-425c-987c-6431040bedfb
---
When designing anything that touches the cross-language data path, avoid these three shapes — the user pushed back on each and the rejections are load-bearing for the architecture.

**1. Don't propose runtime "handles" that route operations back to the originating spec.**

**Why:** Real library functions in the wild (qsort, libcurl, anything from libc) expect actual native values — a real `int (*)(int,int)` function pointer, a real `char *` — not a SoraMech-issued id string. They can't be modified to participate in our runtime's routing scheme. The wire must carry data the destination spec can fully reconstruct into a native value, not opaque tokens.

**How to apply:** When a value seems hard to serialize (function pointers, coroutines, opaque pointers), decompose to data primitives (address + signature, bytecode + upvalues, ptr + length). If the destination needs a callable, the destination spec synthesizes one at reconstruction time (libffi trampoline for C, `ffi.cast` for LuaJIT). The "phone home" callback that the trampoline performs internally is fine — it's an artifact of the destination spec's reconstruction, not exposed in the wire format. What's forbidden is a wire primitive that says "operations on this value cross back to the source."

**2. Don't propose pair-wise language conversion paths (lua→c, c→lua, etc.).**

**Why:** N languages means N² conversion paths, which means adding a new language breaks the existing ones. The wire must use a language-agnostic intermediate (JSON) and every spec must implement only the two pair-symmetric callbacks (`to_json`, `from_json`). New language = implement those two functions, nothing else touches.

**How to apply:** Never write code that names two specific languages in a conversion context. Specs only ever see JSON on the wire. If a JSON primitive needs an extension (like `$function_pointer`), it lives in the universal JSON contract, not as a lua-spec-specific or c-spec-specific concept.

**3. Don't propose per-function annotations for type ambiguities (lengths, discriminators, callback signatures).**

**Why:** The user pointed out that "discriminator" is a vocab word the system has no general way to ask about. We can't prompt "which param is the length?" because that pairing convention lives in the function's English documentation, not its types. Asking the user to write annotations means asking them to know specialized vocabulary they didn't sign up for.

**How to apply:** When a function signature has ambiguous types (`char *` with no length, `union` with no tag, `void *` with no signature), expose each parameter as a separate input slot on the box. The user wires the slots according to the function's documentation — `p` from one source, `n` from another. The system never infers pairings; the user expresses them by wiring. If the destination needs a length and no length wire is provided, the wire compile fails with a plain-English fix-it message ("destination needs length, source provides only a pointer — wire a length input here").

**Common thread:** all three rejections are the same principle in different clothes — **SoraMech bends to the language, not the other way around.** Users write idiomatic code in their language. Libraries are used as-is. Specs are self-contained and pair-symmetric. The system stays out of the user's vocabulary.

```

### project_cross_language_wire.md

```markdown
---
name: Cross-language wire format
description: Dual-ring slot per port (native ring + JSON ring + ordering ring). JSON only at language borders. Per-edge classification at graph load; per-cell precision at runtime.
type: project
originSessionId: 5670858b-274f-49c4-89b8-a4b83ce33d7c
---
Design corrected 2026-05-21 — replaces the older "JSON always" framing.

**Each input slot is a dual-ring buffer.** Per port: one `ring_native` (consumer-language native bytes), one `ring_json` (JSON bytes from cross-language producers), and one `ring_order` of `(which_ring, idx)` tuples in arrival order. Allocate `ring_json` and `ring_order` lazily — same-language-only ports pay nothing for them.

**Push selects ring by per-edge bit.** Producer's spec writes native bytes to `ring_native` if `output_edge_native[j]` is true; otherwise calls `native_to_json` and writes to `ring_json`. Either way, pushes a `(which_ring, idx)` tuple to `ring_order`.

**Pop reads ordering ring first.** Dispatch pops `ring_order`, gets `(which_ring, idx)`, then pops the indicated cell. The consumer's spec receives `(input_data[i], input_native[i])` per input port and dispatches per flag — decode native or decode JSON.

**Why dual-ring instead of per-port classification.** Per-port (slot is all-JSON if any fan-in is cross-lang) is the pessimistic approximation — same-language values eat JSON cost on mixed-fan-in ports. Dual-ring is precise per cell: a port fed by one Lua box and one C box keeps the Lua cells native and JSON-encodes only the C-sourced cells.

**Why JSON for the cross-language ring (not pair-wise paths, not type-annotated raw bytes).** JSON's structure is self-describing — every language has a natural mapping for object/array/number/string/bool/null. Adding language K means K's spec implements its own `native_to_json` and `json_to_native`. N spec-pairs, not N² language-pair paths. The native ring's format is each spec's private choice — Lua may pick msgpack or `cjson.encode` or a custom binary form; C uses `memcpy` of the wrapper struct; Bash uses strings.

**Sentinel primitives for non-JSON-mappable values.** `$ref` (pointer + length into the large-value heap), `$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque` ("these bytes are language-X-internal"). Carried inside the JSON ring as object-shaped sentinels. Cross-language receivers that can't reconstruct fail at wire compile.

**`$function_pointer` reconstruction — wrapper binary + persistent subprocess (issue 318, amended 2026-05-22).** Earlier design had the consumer's spec FFI-cast the raw process-local address. Rejected: FFI is language-pair-specific, biases SoraMech toward the Lua↔C corner, breaks the N-not-N² rule. Replacement: at map-compile time the compiler asks the *producing* spec to compile the referenced function + its enumerable dependencies into a standalone wrapper binary on disk that speaks a small line protocol on stdio. The consumer spec spawns one persistent subprocess per `$function_pointer` it receives and talks to it over the same socket-like channel used for any out-of-process spec. Every language pays the same price; new languages only implement "compile a function to a wrapper binary" — no pair-wise FFI knowledge required.

**Spec `invoke` signature change.** Collapses `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` into one `invoke` with per-input/per-output flags: `int invoke(handle, ..., const int *input_native, int n_inputs, int output_native, void *out_buf, ...)`. The spec dispatches per input internally. This is the contract change that unblocks 306/307's typed marshalling.

**Two compile-time layers stack:**
- **Per-edge classification (issue 312, this design)** — graph loader computes `input_edge_native[]` and `output_edge_native[]` on each box; dispatch routes pushes accordingly. This issue is the wire-format design.
- **Same-language source merge (issue 313, research / blocked)** — concatenate every same-language box's source into one translation unit per language. Boxes still run as separate thread-pool tasks; what 313 thins is the spec invocation overhead inside the merged region (direct call into the worker's `lua_State` instead of dlsym + marshal + bridge). The dual-ring slot machinery does NOT collapse — tasks still need slots to wait for sibling inputs. 313 makes the ring cells cheaper to read/write; it doesn't remove them.

**Three rejected design shapes** (kept here so they don't get reinvented):
1. **Handles back-routing to the owning spec** — library functions in the wild can't be modified to use SoraMech-shaped handles. Rejected.
2. **Pair-wise language paths** — every new language would have to grow N-1 translation paths against existing languages. Rejected. The dual-ring's "two rings per slot" is the dimension-cap that prevents this.
3. **Per-function discriminator / length annotations on the wire** — the system has no universal vocabulary to ask "is param 2 the length?" Rejected; the user expresses these by wiring two separate input slots.

**How to apply:** When discussing wire format, slot store shape, dispatch routing, or spec callbacks: think dual-ring (native, JSON, ordering) per slot, per-edge classification at push time, per-cell tag at pop time, JSON-only-at-borders, language-private native bytes everywhere else.

```

### project_custom_translation_specs.md

```markdown
---
name: Custom translation specs per port
description: Users can override the default native/JSON wire path on any input port for any reason — a knob in the wire format vocabulary (issue 246)
type: project
originSessionId: 1228a221-1c09-4c26-94f3-172d7777b2b9
---
**Per-port custom translation specs** let the user intervene at a specific input port and replace the default dual-ring decode path with user-written code. The override applies to that one port; the rest of the graph uses the default native-or-JSON path from issue 312.

**Motivating cases (any reason qualifies, not just cross-language):**
- Cross-language ambiguity the default specs can't infer: a C++ class with methods landing at a C box (drop methods? expose as fn pointers? restructure?), a `void *` carrying a libcurl handle, a union whose discriminator is on a separate wire.
- Same-language reshape: massaging a value into a different shape on its way in (rename fields, drop a layer, project a subset) without adding a transform box on the canvas.
- Validation / coercion: clamp a number, enforce a schema, fail loudly on unexpected shapes.

**How it composes with the dual-ring design:**
- The custom spec runs at *pop* time on the consumer side, after the ordering ring has been consulted and the cell has been read from the appropriate ring. The user's code receives the decoded value (native or JSON-decoded) and produces whatever the consumer's `invoke` will see.
- A port with a custom translation gets its own wire color in the editor (issue 245) — visible signal that this port doesn't use the default path.

**Why:** The default native/JSON contract covers the cases JSON's structure handles cleanly. Everything outside that needs a user escape hatch — *and* the same hatch is useful for purely-local "I want this input shaped differently" cases. One mechanism, many uses.

**How to apply:** When the design needs an override knob on the cross-language path, propose "custom translation spec on the destination port" rather than inventing per-function annotations, type discriminator metadata, or producer-side conversion hints (all three were rejected — see feedback_cross_language_pitfalls). The port is the unit of override; the spec is user code; the rest stays default.

```

### project_editor_visual_language.md

```markdown
---
name: Editor visual language for wires
description: Wire color in the canvas editor encodes language pairing across the edge, not just routing kind — a UI invariant future work should respect (issue 245)
type: project
originSessionId: 1228a221-1c09-4c26-94f3-172d7777b2b9
---
**Wires are colored by language pairing across the edge**, with a separate color for wires whose destination port carries a user-written custom translation (issue 246). Pre-245 wires were colored purely by routing kind (default slate-blue, comparator branches orange/blue/green).

**The pairing-based palette:**
- **Same-language hop** — the wire carries native bytes; default neutral color.
- **Cross-language hop (forest green, ~`#228b22`)** — different producer/consumer languages; runtime translates via JSON at the wire. The color is a standing reminder that encode + decode is happening on that hop.
- **Custom-translation hop** — a third distinct color signaling the destination port has a user-written translation spec overriding the default path.
- Branch-routing colors (orange/blue/green from comparator boxes) compose on top — routing kind and language pairing are orthogonal axes the editor must show simultaneously.

**Why:** The runtime treats same-lang and cross-lang hops very differently (dual-ring slot design, issue 312), but pre-245 the editor gave the box author no visual signal that the JSON translation step existed. It was invisible until someone read the dispatch code. Translation cost should be legible from the canvas.

**How to apply:** Any future editor work that touches wire rendering must preserve "color encodes language pairing." Don't propose recoloring schemes that overload the cross-language signal onto another concept. New routing concepts get new axes (line style, glyphs, animation) rather than stealing color. When discussing wire visuals, name both axes — routing kind AND language pairing — even if only one is changing.

```

### project_encapsulated_map.md

```markdown
---
name: Encapsulated map as box
description: A sub-map opens as a single box on a parent canvas. Data boxes marked externally-supplied become input ports; write boxes marked externally-consumed become output ports. The documented exception to single-output-per-box (issue 248, amended 2026-05-23)
type: project
originSessionId: 1228a221-1c09-4c26-94f3-172d7777b2b9
---
**A map directory has two ways to be opened:** as the top-level graph (existing map-select UI), or as an **encapsulated box** on the currently-edited parent map (issue 248). Opening through the file browser while a parent map is open instantiates the sub-map as a single box on the parent's canvas.

**The encapsulation contract — symmetric input and output toggles:**
- **Inputs:** any `data` box inside the sub-map can be toggled "externally-supplied" in its inspector. Each such box becomes a typed input port on the encapsulated box.
- **Outputs:** any `write` box inside the sub-map can be toggled "externally-consumed" in its inspector. Each such box surfaces as a **distinct output port** on the encapsulated box. A write box with the toggle on can still write to its `path` if one is set — the file destination and the parent-wire destination are independent.

**Encapsulated maps are the documented exception to issue 218's "single output per box" rule.** They have *N* output ports — one per externally-consumed write box, where *N* may be zero (pure side-effect map), one, or many. Every other box kind still has exactly one output.

**Rejected earlier design (2026-05-21):** packing all write-box outputs into a single byte-blob with a leading null-terminated size index, single wire out. Rejected 2026-05-23 because it would have repurposed file-writing machinery to emit strings into wires — substituting one mechanism for another. The symmetric toggle design preserves the `write` box's "terminate a value" semantics and treats the destination kind as a mode toggle.

**Why:** Encapsulation is a function-like abstraction over a sub-graph. The parent doesn't see the sub-map's internals — it sees declared input ports, declared output ports, and known semantics. Sub-maps can be edited independently, reused across parents, and packaged once for distribution. This is the closest SoraMech gets to "user-defined functions" while staying graph-native.

**How to apply:** When discussing map composition, reuse, or "how do users build libraries of behavior" — encapsulation is the answer. Don't propose alternative function-abstraction mechanisms (named subgraphs as text references, macro-style inlining, blob-packed outputs) without explicitly weighing them against the symmetric-toggle shape. Both toggles (`externally-supplied` on data boxes, `externally-consumed` on write boxes) are the user-facing port-declaration mechanism — don't invent a separate "ports manifest" file.

```

### project_soramech.md

```markdown
---
name: SoraMech project context
description: Core architecture decisions, file layout, and execution model for SoraMech, across phase 2 (current) and phase 3 (designed)
type: project
originSessionId: 579d58b3-7452-44f3-983a-80ff5821082a
---
SoraMech is a visual FSM/dataflow editor and execution engine at /mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/.

**Why:** Primary use case is LLM orchestration on the SoraMind Alpine cluster. Strings flow between boxes like variables. History is explicit, loaded from disk only when a box needs it.

**Three independent programs sharing a map directory:**
- `src/006-server-main.lua` — thin HTTP file CRUD proxy. No runner management.
- Runner — phase 2: `src/007-runner-main.lua` (synchronous Lua). Phase 3: `soramech-pool` (C binary, designed in issues 301–311, not yet built).
- `assets/index.html` — vanilla JS canvas editor. Editor only — no run button. Compile button (issue 222) is a phase 2 placeholder; it triggers the phase 3 build pipeline (issue 219).

**Key architecture decisions (current truth):**
- Each box is its own JSON file in `boxes/<id>.json`. Connections written to BOTH endpoint files; loader validates both ends agree — hard error if not.
- **Single output wire per box** (issue 218 complete). No `outputs` array. The function returns one value; routing is by `from_branch` on the connection.
- **Comparator-based branching** (issue 210) — replaces the earlier `branch` box kind with named ports. A box may carry a `comparand`; the dispatch layer fires `lt`/`eq`/`gt` connections. No spec is invoked for comparators.
- **Iterator boxes** (issue 221) — pure routing primitives, no `ref`/`fn`. Dispatch layer cycles through `iterator_outputs` based on a per-task counter.
- **Phase 2 drivers** are shell scripts under `drivers/` (one process per call, stdout as channel). **Phase 3 language specs** are `.so` libraries under `langs/<name>/spec.so` loaded via `dlopen` (issue 303). Lua and C are in-process; Bash is out-of-process via Unix domain socket.
- Data files: per-map JSON in `data/`. Section-level `constant` flag. Accessed via `libs/soramech-data.lua`.
- State/ephemeral: `tmp/` is a symlink to `/tmp/<map-name>/`. Phase 2 writes `last-run.json` (single doc). Phase 3 writes `last-run.jsonl` (JSON Lines, issue 311) — decoupled from the editor, consumed by LLMs / CLI viewers / test harness.
- **Phase 3 runtime model**: C pool runner owns a 3d-rts task pool; per-task slots in process heap (issue 302) with refcounting and a separate large-value heap; dispatch layer (304) is the worker action; graph loader is C (305), no embedded Lua at the runner level. Phase 2's synchronous executor retires when phase 3 lands.

**How to apply:** When working on any component, respect the three-way separation. Server never knows about runner. Runner never knows about server. Map directory format is the only shared contract. The phase 2 driver model and the phase 3 spec model coexist conceptually (phase 2 is current, phase 3 is fully designed) but the runtime mechanism differs entirely; box JSON and the editor are unchanged across the cutover.

```

### project_threading.md

```markdown
---
name: threading_model
description: SoraMech's threading model — phase 2 synchronous, phase 3 SoraMech-owned C thread pool with per-port slots and language specs
type: project
originSessionId: 579d58b3-7452-44f3-983a-80ff5821082a
---
**Phase 2 (current)**: synchronous executor in `src/004-executor.lua`. Single-threaded, sequential box execution. One driver shell-out per box call. The phase 2 path goes away when phase 3 lands — no fallback, no coroutine intermediate.

**Phase 3 (designed, not yet built)**: C pool runner with a SoraMech-owned thread pool. The 3d-rts pool at `/home/ritz/programming/ai-stuff/games/3d-rts/libs/900-task-pool.h` is the **design reference**, not vendored — SoraMech owns its pool implementation so the spawn primitive, worker context, init barrier, and iterator-pinning extensions are all under our control. The architecture is fully specified across issues 301–313.

Key phase 3 concepts:
- **Per-input-port slots** (not per-task), allocated at graph load and durable for the run. Each slot is a ring buffer (`n_cells` cells of `cell_capacity` bytes). 1-cell slots use peek-not-drain (literals, single-push wires); N-cell slots use pop-and-drain (queued / multi-push wires). No per-slot refcounting — slot lifetime is the whole run.
- **Tasks are ephemeral**: spawned dynamically by the dispatch layer when a box's input set is ready. Tasks carry just `box_id` and a counter snapshot. Box state (counter, input slots, language handle) is durable.
- **Language specs** under `langs/<name>/spec.so`, loaded via `dlopen` at startup. Uniform `lang_spec_t`. No language is privileged.
- **Same-language fast path** (issue 312): wire format defaults to JSON, but two adjacent same-language boxes use the language's native serialization (Lua `lua_dump`, C struct memcpy). Compile-time wire classification picks the path.
- **In-process specs** (Lua, C) via `lua_pcall` / `dlsym`. **Out-of-process specs** (Bash) via Unix domain sockets to a persistent per-worker subprocess.
- **Dispatch layer** (304) is the worker-side action: pop inputs → branch on box mode → invoke spec → push to downstream input slots → end. No blocking, no wait lists; tasks spawn on input arrival and run to completion.
- **Iterator and comparator routing** in the dispatch layer. Comparator runs the function and routes by output value. Iterator skips the function and routes by per-box counter (atomic spawn-time increment). Iterator tasks parallelize across workers; ordering preserved via per-push counter tags on consumer slots.
- **No-null rule** (issue 230): every input port must be wired, given a literal, or marked optional. Compile fails on missing inputs.
- **Init barrier**: pool startup waits until every worker has run every spec's `init(worker_idx)` before submitting tasks. Avoids tasks landing on uninitialized handles.
- **Hard-crash error policy**: nonzero return from any spec aborts the program.
- **Blocking semantics**: long-running operations tie up a worker. Pool size is the budget. Cooperative split (kickoff + check-done iterator) avoids holding a worker. See `docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md`.

**Effil is explicitly NOT used.** Coroutines are not used. The C pool is the chosen mechanism. 3d-rts is referenced as design inspiration, not vendored.

**How to apply:** When discussing threading, parallelism, or cross-language IPC: phase 2 is synchronous shell-out drivers; phase 3 is the SoraMech-owned C pool runner with `lang_spec_t`, per-port slot stores, and dispatch-layer routing. Never suggest effil. 3d-rts is a reference, not a dependency. Issues 301–313 carry the design.

```

### reference_strategems.md

```markdown
---
name: Strategems directory
description: Cross-cutting design rules the user has crystallized into named files at strategems/ — re-list and consider once per context
type: reference
originSessionId: 1228a221-1c09-4c26-94f3-172d7777b2b9
---
**Location:** `/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/strategems/`

**What it is:** Files in this directory are **distilled design rules** the user has named after a pattern that recurs in many places. They're shorter than docs/, longer than a one-line CLAUDE.md instruction, and load-bearing for design judgment. Each file is one strategem — a rule with a memorable phrase as the filename. The phrase-as-filename is the strategem's name; use it as shared vocabulary.

**Current strategems (as of 2026-05-23):**
- `commit-language-is-runtime-language.md`
- `contracts-must-fit-every-implementer.md`
- `redesigns-change-the-surface-too.md`

**How to apply (the user's preference is encouragement, not force):**
- **Re-list the directory once per context** (`ls strategems/`) so the current set is in my working memory. Don't auto-read each one — let the filenames sit there as triggers.
- When a filename *feels* relevant to the task at hand, read it before proceeding. Relevance signals: I'm about to propose a contract, a redesign, a language-spec change, or anything where a recurring principle might apply. If nothing feels relevant, don't force a reach.
- Re-list at session start (or first time the project surfaces) — new strategems land here without ceremony and the list above may be stale across sessions.
- When a strategem does apply, name it explicitly to the user using its filename phrase. That's the shared vocabulary the naming is for.
- If during a session the user expresses a rule in the form "X must always Y because Z" and the rule generalizes beyond the current task, suggest writing it as a strategem rather than only as a memory.

**Recent commit pattern:** strategem files are committed individually (e.g. `c57c719 strategems: contracts must fit every implementer, not just the simplest`), so `git log -- strategems/` is the fastest way to see what's been added lately and the reasoning that prompted it.

```

    "content":"- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- frontend-design:frontend-design: Create distinctive, production-grade frontend interfaces with high design quality. Use this skill when the user asks to build web components, pages, or applications. Generates creative, polished code that avoids generic AI aesthetics.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch"

        "content":"1\t/* langs/lang-spec.h — public interface for SoraMech language specs.\n2\t *\n3\t * What it is, in a sentence: the C contract that every language\n4\t * spec (Lua, C, Bash, anything user-added) implements. The pool\n5\t * runner discovers specs by dlopen()ing langs/<name>/spec.so at\n6\t * startup and reading the exported soramech_lang_spec symbol.\n7\t *\n8\t * Designed in issue 303. The init barrier (issue 301) calls init()\n9\t * for every spec on every worker before unblocking task dispatch;\n10\t * the dispatch action (issue 304) calls invoke() per task; the\n11\t * compile-button pipeline (issue 309) calls compile() per source\n12\t * file for languages whose compile pointer is non-NULL.\n13\t *\n14\t * Errors: a nonzero return from any callback is fatal. The dispatch\n15\t * layer prints the error context and aborts. There is no recovery,\n16\t * no retry, no per-task failed state. This is intentional.\n17\t *\n18\t * Issue 312 extends this contract with optional fast-path\n19\t * callbacks: `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` are specialised\n20\t * variants the dispatch layer prefers when wire classification\n21\t * says they apply; `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are\n22\t * bridges used at language boundaries. Specs that don't set them\n23\t * fall back to `invoke` for every call.\n24\t */\n25\t\n26\t#ifndef SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H\n27\t#define SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H\n28\t\n29\t#include <stddef.h>\n30\t\n31\t#ifdef __cplusplus\n32\textern \"C\" {\n33\t#endif\n34\t\n35\t/* Forward declaration. `box_t` lives in src/010-graph-loader.h and\n36\t * carries the full per-box record (input declarations, language,\n37\t * compile-time hints like `cflags` / `link_libs` / `headers`).\n38\t * Spec implementations include the loader header when they want to\n39\t * read those fields; the bare forward declaration here keeps the\n40\t * spec interface stand-alone for callers that only need the typedef\n41\t * names. */\n42\ttypedef struct box box_t;\n43\t\n44\t/* {{{ Shared signatures */\n45\t/* Invoke signature — shared by `invoke`, `invoke_native`, and\n46\t * `invoke_json`. The dispatch layer picks which one to call based\n47\t * on the wire-format classification in issue 312.\n48\t *\n49\t * Per-input format flag (`input_native[i]`): non-zero iff the\n50\t * bytes in `input_data[i]` are this spec's native form; zero iff\n51\t * they are JSON from a cross-language producer. The spec\n52\t * dispatches internally — its decoder for that input picks the\n53\t * native or JSON path based on the flag.\n54\t *\n55\t * Per-output format flag (`output_native`): non-zero iff the\n56\t * dispatch wants the spec to write native bytes to `out_buf`,\n57\t * zero iff it wants JSON. The spec produces one form per call;\n58\t * cross-language fan-out is handled by the dispatch layer, which\n59\t * converts via `native_to_json` once and pushes the JSON to every\n60\t * cross-language consumer.\n61\t *\n62\t * On non-dual-ring slots (single-language inputs or single-language\n63\t * outputs only), the dispatch passes NULL for `input_native` and\n64\t * 0 for `output_native`; the spec falls back to its default\n65\t * decode (typically JSON, which Lua's existing bridge handles).\n66\t * `input_native` may be NULL even when `n_inputs > 0` — specs\n67\t * must check before dereferencing. */\n68\ttypedef int (*lang_invoke_fn)(void *handle,\n69\t                              const box_t *box,\n70\t                              const char *file_path,\n71\t                              const char *fn_name,\n72\t                              const void **input_data,\n73\t                              const int   *input_sizes,\n74\t                              const int   *input_native,\n75\t                              int          n_inputs,\n76\t                              int          output_native,\n77\t                              void        *out_buf,\n78\t                              int          out_buf_capacity,\n79\t                              int         *out_size);\n80\t\n81\t/* `box` is the box record being invoked, or NULL when the caller has\n82\t * no box context (e.g. test fixtures driving the spec directly).\n83\t * Specs that need declared return type, compile hints, or future\n84\t * sentinel-emission decisions read fields off `box`. Specs that\n85\t * don't need it ignore the parameter. The pointer is valid only for\n86\t * the duration of the call. */\n87\t\n88\t/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.\n89\t * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one\n90\t * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as\n91\t * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default).\n92\t *\n93\t * `handle` is the worker's per-language handle (the same one\n94\t * `invoke` receives) — bridges may need it to reach a runtime-side\n95\t * scratch area: Lua reads/writes via the lua_State stack, C may\n96\t * keep a typed-pointer table, Bash may need its sub-process. Specs\n97\t * that don't need it ignore the argument.\n98\t *\n99\t * Stack-protocol convention for `native_to_json`: the producer\n100\t * leaves its native value on top of its runtime's working store\n101\t * (Lua: top of stack); the bridge consumes it. For `json_to_native`:\n102\t * the bridge pushes the reconstructed value onto the same store.\n103\t * `src` / `dst` carry only the JSON side. */\n104\ttypedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(void *handle,\n105\t                              const void *src, int src_size,\n106\t                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);\n107\t/* }}} */\n108\t\n109\t/* {{{ lang_spec_t */\n110\ttypedef struct lang_spec {\n111\t    const char *name;       /* \"lua\", \"bash\", \"c\", \"python\", ... */\n112\t    const char *file_ext;   /* \".lua\", \".sh\", \".c\", \".py\"        */\n113\t\n114\t    /* init: once per worker thread at pool startup. Returns an\n115\t     * opaque handle stored in the worker context; the dispatch\n116\t     * layer passes this handle back to invoke() for every call. */\n117\t    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);\n118\t\n119\t    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle\n120\t     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,\n121\t     * persistent socket connections). */\n122\t    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);\n123\t\n124\t    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load\n125\t     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't\n126\t     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs\n127\t     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path, plus any per-box\n128\t     * compile flags / link libraries / headers the box declared.\n129\t     *\n130\t     * `box` is the box record for the box being compiled, or NULL\n131\t     * if the caller is compiling a source file with no box context\n132\t     * (e.g. the C spec's internal lazy-compile path that maps a raw\n133\t     * `.c` ref to a `/tmp/<hash>.so` on the fly). When non-NULL,\n134\t     * the spec may read box->cflags / box->link_libs / box->headers\n135\t     * to customise the compile. Returns 0 on success, nonzero on\n136\t     * failure. */\n137\t    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path,\n138\t                     const box_t *box);\n139\t\n140\t    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,\n141\t     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the\n142\t     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.\n143\t     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.\n144\t     *\n145\t     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.\n146\t     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised\n147\t     * paths below are not set. */\n148\t    lang_invoke_fn invoke;\n149\t\n150\t    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire\n151\t     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this\n152\t     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's\n153\t     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct\n154\t     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.\n155\t     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls\n156\t     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */\n157\t    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;\n158\t\n159\t    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the\n160\t     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;\n161\t     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */\n162\t    lang_invoke_fn invoke_json;\n163\t\n164\t    /* Bridges between native and JSON wire forms. Used when a\n165\t     * value crosses a language boundary — the producer writes\n166\t     * native, the dispatch layer converts via the producer's\n167\t     * native_to_json, hands the JSON bytes to the consumer's\n168\t     * json_to_native. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;\n169\t     * dispatch treats native and JSON as identical bytes. Issue\n170\t     * 312. */\n171\t    lang_bridge_fn native_to_json;\n172\t    lang_bridge_fn json_to_native;\n173\t} lang_spec_t;\n174\t/* }}} */\n175\t\n176\t#ifdef __cplusplus\n177\t}\n178\t#endif\n179\t\n180\t#endif /* SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H */\n181\t",

  "toolUseResult":{
    "type":"text",
    "file":{
      "content":"/* langs/lang-spec.h — public interface for SoraMech language specs.\n *\n * What it is, in a sentence: the C contract that every language\n * spec (Lua, C, Bash, anything user-added) implements. The pool\n * runner discovers specs by dlopen()ing langs/<name>/spec.so at\n * startup and reading the exported soramech_lang_spec symbol.\n *\n * Designed in issue 303. The init barrier (issue 301) calls init()\n * for every spec on every worker before unblocking task dispatch;\n * the dispatch action (issue 304) calls invoke() per task; the\n * compile-button pipeline (issue 309) calls compile() per source\n * file for languages whose compile pointer is non-NULL.\n *\n * Errors: a nonzero return from any callback is fatal. The dispatch\n * layer prints the error context and aborts. There is no recovery,\n * no retry, no per-task failed state. This is intentional.\n *\n * Issue 312 extends this contract with optional fast-path\n * callbacks: `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` are specialised\n * variants the dispatch layer prefers when wire classification\n * says they apply; `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are\n * bridges used at language boundaries. Specs that don't set them\n * fall back to `invoke` for every call.\n */\n\n#ifndef SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H\n#define SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H\n\n#include <stddef.h>\n\n#ifdef __cplusplus\nextern \"C\" {\n#endif\n\n/* Forward declaration. `box_t` lives in src/010-graph-loader.h and\n * carries the full per-box record (input declarations, language,\n * compile-time hints like `cflags` / `link_libs` / `headers`).\n * Spec implementations include the loader header when they want to\n * read those fields; the bare forward declaration here keeps the\n * spec interface stand-alone for callers that only need the typedef\n * names. */\ntypedef struct box box_t;\n\n/* {{{ Shared signatures */\n/* Invoke signature — shared by `invoke`, `invoke_native`, and\n * `invoke_json`. The dispatch layer picks which one to call based\n * on the wire-format classification in issue 312.\n *\n * Per-input format flag (`input_native[i]`): non-zero iff the\n * bytes in `input_data[i]` are this spec's native form; zero iff\n * they are JSON from a cross-language producer. The spec\n * dispatches internally — its decoder for that input picks the\n * native or JSON path based on the flag.\n *\n * Per-output format flag (`output_native`): non-zero iff the\n * dispatch wants the spec to write native bytes to `out_buf`,\n * zero iff it wants JSON. The spec produces one form per call;\n * cross-language fan-out is handled by the dispatch layer, which\n * converts via `native_to_json` once and pushes the JSON to every\n * cross-language consumer.\n *\n * On non-dual-ring slots (single-language inputs or single-language\n * outputs only), the dispatch passes NULL for `input_native` and\n * 0 for `output_native`; the spec falls back to its default\n * decode (typically JSON, which Lua's existing bridge handles).\n * `input_native` may be NULL even when `n_inputs > 0` — specs\n * must check before dereferencing. */\ntypedef int (*lang_invoke_fn)(void *handle,\n                              const box_t *box,\n                              const char *file_path,\n                              const char *fn_name,\n                              const void **input_data,\n                              const int   *input_sizes,\n                              const int   *input_native,\n                              int          n_inputs,\n                              int          output_native,\n                              void        *out_buf,\n                              int          out_buf_capacity,\n                              int         *out_size);\n\n/* `box` is the box record being invoked, or NULL when the caller has\n * no box context (e.g. test fixtures driving the spec directly).\n * Specs that need declared return type, compile hints, or future\n * sentinel-emission decisions read fields off `box`. Specs that\n * don't need it ignore the parameter. The pointer is valid only for\n * the duration of the call. */\n\n/* Wire-format bridge: convert one direction of native ↔ JSON.\n * Used when a value crosses a language boundary. Spec sets one\n * or both; if NULL, the dispatch layer treats native and JSON as\n * identical byte streams (the current uniform-byte default).\n *\n * `handle` is the worker's per-language handle (the same one\n * `invoke` receives) — bridges may need it to reach a runtime-side\n * scratch area: Lua reads/writes via the lua_State stack, C may\n * keep a typed-pointer table, Bash may need its sub-process. Specs\n * that don't need it ignore the argument.\n *\n * Stack-protocol convention for `native_to_json`: the producer\n * leaves its native value on top of its runtime's working store\n * (Lua: top of stack); the bridge consumes it. For `json_to_native`:\n * the bridge pushes the reconstructed value onto the same store.\n * `src` / `dst` carry only the JSON side. */\ntypedef int (*lang_bridge_fn)(void *handle,\n                              const void *src, int src_size,\n                              void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);\n/* }}} */\n\n/* {{{ lang_spec_t */\ntypedef struct lang_spec {\n    const char *name;       /* \"lua\", \"bash\", \"c\", \"python\", ... */\n    const char *file_ext;   /* \".lua\", \".sh\", \".c\", \".py\"        */\n\n    /* init: once per worker thread at pool startup. Returns an\n     * opaque handle stored in the worker context; the dispatch\n     * layer passes this handle back to invoke() for every call. */\n    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);\n\n    /* teardown: once per worker at pool shutdown. Frees the handle\n     * and any per-worker resources (e.g. lua_State, dlopen caches,\n     * persistent socket connections). */\n    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);\n\n    /* compile: optional. Called once per source file at map-load\n     * (or at editor compile-button time). For languages that don't\n     * compile (Lua, Bash), leave this NULL. For C, it runs\n     * gcc -shared -fPIC -o out_path src_path, plus any per-box\n     * compile flags / link libraries / headers the box declared.\n     *\n     * `box` is the box record for the box being compiled, or NULL\n     * if the caller is compiling a source file with no box context\n     * (e.g. the C spec's internal lazy-compile path that maps a raw\n     * `.c` ref to a `/tmp/<hash>.so` on the fly). When non-NULL,\n     * the spec may read box->cflags / box->link_libs / box->headers\n     * to customise the compile. Returns 0 on success, nonzero on\n     * failure. */\n    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path,\n                     const box_t *box);\n\n    /* invoke: per box call. Inputs are arrays of (bytes, size) pairs,\n     * already read from slots by the dispatch layer. Returns the\n     * function's output by writing to out_buf and setting *out_size.\n     * Returns 0 on success, nonzero (fatal) on error.\n     *\n     * This is the canonical entry point; every spec implements it.\n     * The dispatch layer falls back to it when the specialised\n     * paths below are not set. */\n    lang_invoke_fn invoke;\n\n    /* invoke_native — optional fast path used when every wire\n     * adjacent to this box (input and output) stays inside this\n     * spec's language. Bytes on the wire are this language's\n     * native serialisation (Lua tables via msgpack, C struct\n     * memcpy, etc.) and the encode/decode round trip is skipped.\n     * NULL means the spec hasn't specialised; dispatch falls\n     * back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */\n    lang_invoke_fn invoke_native;\n\n    /* invoke_json — optional universal-interop path. Bytes on the\n     * wire are JSON. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;\n     * dispatch falls back to `invoke`. Issue 312. */\n    lang_invoke_fn invoke_json;\n\n    /* Bridges between native and JSON wire forms. Used when a\n     * value crosses a language boundary — the producer writes\n     * native, the dispatch layer converts via the producer's\n     * native_to_json, hands the JSON bytes to the consumer's\n     * json_to_native. NULL means the spec hasn't specialised;\n     * dispatch treats native and JSON as identical bytes. Issue\n     * 312. */\n    lang_bridge_fn native_to_json;\n    lang_bridge_fn json_to_native;\n} lang_spec_t;\n/* }}} */\n\n#ifdef __cplusplus\n}\n#endif\n\n#endif /* SORAMECH_LANG_SPEC_H */\n",
      "totalLines":181,
      "filePath":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech/langs/lang-spec.h",

Let me synthesize the spec contract changes from the issues:\n\n## Language Spec Contract Changes from Planned Issues\n\nBased on the 13 issues reviewed, here are the NEW responsibilities that will be added to or CHANGED in the `lang_spec_t` contract:\n\n**Issue 246 — Custom Translation Specs Per Port**\n- **Custom decode on input ports**: The dispatch gains a per-port branch that calls a user-supplied custom translation function before passing input to `invoke`. The spec remains unchanged but must accept an additional `input_native` parameter that the custom shim also receives—specs need to handle cases where custom shims have already decoded the input and mark it as native via the flag.\n\n**Issue 312 — Same-Language Wire Fast Path**\n- **`input_native[]` array in invoke signature** (already reserved in header): The spec now *must* dispatch internally on `input_native[i]` per input. No longer can assume all inputs are one format.\n- **`output_native` flag** (already reserved): The spec must produce either native or JSON bytes in one form per call, selected by this flag.\n- **`native_to_json` and `json_to_native` bridges are now *required*** (not optional): Every spec must implement both or stub with hard-error. These drive cross-language wire translation and data-box semantics.\n\n**Issue 317 — Language Spec JSON Bridge for Data Boxes**\n- **Data box language execution**: Specs gain responsibility for executing `.lua`/`.c`/`.sh` files in data boxes and returning native values that `native_to_json` then converts. The dispatch will call the spec to load and run the file, not just push literal bytes.\n\n**Issue 318 — Sentinel Primitives in Cross-Language JSON**\n- **Sentinel emission on `native_to_json`**: The spec must emit three sentinel kinds (`$ref`, `$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque`) when native values cannot be structurally represented in JSON. Lua must replace hard-errors on closures/userdata with sentinel emission.\n- **Sentinel reconstruction on `json_to_native`**: The spec must recognize and reconstruct sentinels, raising compile-time errors when the consumer language cannot handle the sentinel kind.\n- **Per-spec capability declarations**: Each spec must declare which sentinel kinds it can emit and which it can reconstruct.\n\n**Issue 248 — Encapsulated Map as Box**\n- **New language \"map\"**: A new spec (`langs/map/spec.so`) joins the registry with `lang: \"map\"` boxes. Its `init` loads sub-maps, `invoke` runs the sub-graph and packs outputs into the null-terminated size-index format, and `compile` either inlines or validates the sub-graph.\n\n**Issue 313 — Whole-Program Same-Language Merge**\n- **Merged-module loading (Lua, future C/Bash)**: The spec gains `load_via_merged()` on the cache-miss path—when `__merged__.<ext>` exists alongside the source file, the spec loads the merged module once per worker and indexes it by basename. Direct function calls inside the merged region bypass the spec's `invoke` path entirely (dispatch optimization, not spec responsibility, but specs need awareness of the merge).\n\nAll other issues (245, 247, 249, 244, 304, 315, 311) touch the editor, runtime dispatch, or tooling—they do not change the spec contract itself."

```json
{
  "timestamp":"2026-05-22T18:49:26.168Z",
  "entrypoint":"cli",
  "gitBranch":"master",
  "version":"2.1.121",
  "uuid":"2bd1852b-2e13-43be-a043-c85642fb0b6f",
  "parentUuid":"74c745b1-2168-4ad0-beb6-b2ae89d4a9ad",
  "sessionId":"8ec29876-d560-4fbc-934b-227cde0b3a68",

  "requestId":"req_011CbJA957sm8ERYYB7XkiAx",
  "cwd":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech",
  "message":{
    "content":[{
        "type":"text",
        "text":"# Language Spec Responsibilities — The Planned End State\n\nBelow is the consolidated list, blending today's `lang-spec.h` contract with what the open issues will require. I've grouped by responsibility, not by issue.\n\n## A. Lifecycle & registration\n- **Identify itself**: provide `name` (`\"lua\"`, `\"c\"`, ...) and `file_ext` so the loader can dlopen `langs/<name>/spec.so` and resolve source files.\n- **Per-worker init**: stand up the runtime (lua_State, dlopen cache, bash subprocess) once per worker thread, return an opaque handle.\n- **Per-worker teardown**: free everything the handle owns at pool shutdown.\n\n## B. Source → executable\n- **Compile a source file** (optional, NULL if not applicable) into an artifact the spec can later invoke, honoring per-box `cflags` / `link_libs` / `headers` when a `box_t` is passed.\n- **Lazy/no-box compile** for ad-hoc references (the C spec's `/tmp/<hash>.so` path).\n\n## C. The three invoke variants (issue 312)\n- **`invoke` (canonical)**: per-call entry; reads each input's `input_native[i]` flag and dispatches internally to its native or JSON decoder; writes output in the form requested by `output_native`.\n- **`invoke_native` (optional fast path)**: all neighboring wires are same-language; bytes are this spec's native serialisation, no encode/decode round-trip.\n- **`invoke_json` (optional universal path)**: all wires are JSON.\n\n## D. Wire-format bridges (issues 312, 318)\n- **`native_to_json`**: drain the top of the runtime's working store, emit JSON bytes. Becomes *required*, not optional, once 312 lands.\n- **`json_to_native`**: parse JSON bytes, push reconstructed value onto the runtime's working store.\n- **Sentinel emission**: when a native value isn't structurally JSON-representable, emit one of `$ref`, `$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque` instead of erroring.\n- **Sentinel reconstruction**: recognise those sentinels on the inbound side and rebuild a runtime value, or raise a compile-time error if this language can't host that kind.\n- **Capability declaration**: each spec declares which sentinel kinds it can emit and which it can reconstruct, so cross-language wires can be validated before run.\n\n## E. Data-box execution (issue 317)\n- **Run a source file as a value producer**: load `.lua` / `.c` / `.sh` from a data box, execute it, hand back a native value, and let `native_to_json` finish the trip onto the wire.\n\n## F. Custom per-port translation (issue 246)\n- **Cooperate with user-supplied port shims**: when a shim has pre-decoded an input, `invoke` must trust the `input_native[i]` flag the shim set, rather than re-decoding.\n\n## G. Encapsulated-map spec (issue 248)\n- A new `langs/map/spec.so` joins the registry as a peer language. Its `invoke` runs a sub-graph; its `compile` either inlines the sub-graph or validates it. (This is a new spec, not a new responsibility for existing ones — but it widens what \"a language spec\" means.)\n\n## H. Whole-program merged loading (issue 313, research)\n- **Awareness of `__merged__.<ext>`**: on cache miss, if a merged module exists alongside source, load it once per worker and index by basename. Calls inside the merged region bypass `invoke` entirely — the spec must be okay with the dispatch layer reaching past it.\n\n## Not the spec's job (clarifying the boundary)\nThe following open issues touch the surrounding system but do **not** add spec responsibilities: 245 (wire color in editor), 247 (debug build / stats lens), 249 (tap input UI), 244 (pull-on-demand data boxes — dispatch concern), 304 (dispatch action itself), 311 (integration tests), 315 (artifact refcounting — done outside the spec).\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- The spec contract is migrating from \"decode JSON, run, encode JSON\" toward \"be a translator between a runtime's native value store and a shared JSON wire.\" After 312+318 land, **the bridges are the heart of the spec**, not `invoke` — `invoke` becomes a thin shell that delegates to the bridges based on flags.\n- Sentinel capability declarations (H) are the first piece of *meta* responsibility: the spec describes what it *can* do, not just *what to do*. That shape — declared capabilities consulted at graph-load — is likely to recur (e.g. future \"can stream\", \"can run pure\", \"can be merged\").\n- Issue 248's `map` spec quietly establishes that \"language\" is just \"anything that satisfies the `lang_spec_t` contract.\" Once a non-textual, non-compiled thing is a spec, the contract itself becomes the abstraction boundary of SoraMech — which is the layer you'll want to design *next* against.\n`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`\n\nA natural question to chew on while you plan: once the bridges carry the real work and `invoke` is mostly a flag-router, is there still a reason for three separate invoke entry points, or does that collapse into one `invoke(flags...)` with the fast paths becoming an internal optimisation the spec — not the dispatch — chooses?"
      }],
    "role":"assistant",
    "id":"msg_01GkvDe4Fp1nxcNch8zik3VF",
    "type":"message",
    "model":"claude-opus-4-7",
    "stop_reason":"end_turn",

The three invoke variants (issue 312)\n\n  - invoke (canonical): per-call entry; reads each input's input_native[i] flag and dispatches\n  internally to its native or JSON decoder; writes output in the form requested by output_native.\n  - invoke_native (optional fast path): all neighboring wires are same-language; bytes are this\n  spec's native serialisation, no encode/decode round-trip.\n  - invoke_json (optional universal path): all wires are JSON.\n\n\ncan you tell me why we have invoke, and then invoke_(native|json)? why not drop the canonical invoke, and always route to the correct of the other two types? Is there a valid reason for that extra option?\n\n  - Sentinel emission: when a native value isn't structurally JSON-representable, emit one of\n  $ref, $function_pointer, $lang_opaque instead of erroring.\n\nmy understanding for the function pointers, and other similar datum, was that we were going to take the function that is pointed at (and all of it's dependencies, if any) and compile it, on-the-fly if necessary but preferrably during the map's compile stage. Then, we were going to ask the language spec to write a little wrapper that runs a piece of code from another language - essentially every language can ask the OS to execute a binary with certain arguments, so if we can write into each spec how specifically to generate such wrappers, then we can successfully call other functions and such in other languages.\n\n  E. Data-box execution (issue 317)\n\n  - Run a source file as a value producer: load .lua / .c / .sh from a data box, execute it, hand\n  back a native value, and let native_to_json finish the trip onto the wire.\n\n\nHmmmmm..... how is that different than a call box? Its invoke runs a sub-graph;\n  its compile either inlines the sub-graph or validates it. ```json
{
  "timestamp":"2026-05-22T19:06:24.424Z",
  "entrypoint":"cli",
  "gitBranch":"master",
  "version":"2.1.121",
  "uuid":"9a7ee7c2-42ca-4a54-909e-243ea803a6d9",
  "parentUuid":"8522fe08-3776-4988-bc76-1245c782266f",

  "requestId":"req_011CbJBPqphpcgnJTHrkLFi6",
  "cwd":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech",
  "message":{
    "content":[{
        "type":"text",
        "text":"Good probing questions — let me answer each, and a few I'll bounce back because the design call is yours.\n\n## 1. Why `invoke` alongside `invoke_native` / `invoke_json`?\n\nThe two fast paths handle the *uniform* cases: every input native, or every input JSON. They don't handle a third case that genuinely exists: **a single call where some inputs are native and others are JSON** (a Lua box reading from one Lua producer and one C producer). That's the case the canonical `invoke` covers, by routing per-input using the `input_native[i]` flag.\n\nYou *could* eliminate it — but only by mandating that the dispatch layer always normalise inputs to one form before the call (e.g. \"if any input is JSON, convert everything to JSON, call `invoke_json`\"). That's a real design choice with a real cost: you'd be doing one needless `native_to_json` per non-matching input on every mixed call.\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- `invoke` isn't really a third *variant* — it's the **general form** that the other two are *specialisations* of. The two fast paths exist because they let the spec skip per-input branching and per-call format checks in the hot loop.\n- A simpler contract would be: spec implements only `invoke`, and the dispatch layer hints \"all native\" or \"all JSON\" via the flags. The spec internally chooses the cheap path. That trades a few branches for one fewer entry point.\n─────────────────────────────────────────────────\n\n**Question back:** is the hot-loop branch cost worth a second entry point to you, or would you rather collapse to one `invoke(flags…)` and let the spec author decide internally?\n\n## 2. Function pointers — your model vs. issue 318's sentinel\n\nYou're describing something quite different from 318, and frankly more concrete. Let me play it back:\n\n> \"Compile the pointed-at function and its dependencies at map compile time. Then ask each language spec for a recipe: how does *your* language shell out to a binary with arguments? Generate a wrapper from that recipe.\"\n\nThat's a **`generate_foreign_call_wrapper(target_lang, fn_signature)`** responsibility — each spec knows how *it* shells out (Lua: `io.popen`, C: `execve`, Bash: literal subshell), and the compiler stamps wrappers at boundary crossings. The wire just carries the path to the compiled binary plus arguments.\n\nIssue 318 currently says the spec emits a `$function_pointer` sentinel and the consumer reconstructs it. That's much vaguer — it doesn't say *how* the consumer turns the sentinel back into something callable.\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- Your design moves the work to compile time and makes it visible (a wrapper is a real file you can read); the sentinel design defers it to runtime and hides it in the bridge. The former is more debuggable and aligns with the \"specs are translators\" framing already emerging.\n- It also means a function-pointer crossing isn't really \"cross-language\" at the data layer at all — it's *two same-language calls* with a process boundary in the middle. That's a strictly simpler mental model.\n─────────────────────────────────────────────────\n\n**Question back:** should I open an amendment to 318 that replaces `$function_pointer` sentinel-passing with the compile-time wrapper-generation approach? Want me to write that up so you can review?\n\n## 3. Data-box-execution vs. call-box — is it the same thing?\n\nYou're right to be suspicious. A call box runs a function and emits its return; a \"source file in a data box\" runs a function and emits its return. The only mechanical differences I can think of:\n\n- Data boxes have no input ports (zero-arg call).\n- Data boxes might be evaluated lazily / on pull (issue 244) rather than on every tick.\n- Data boxes were originally meant to hold *literal* values, not code.\n\nMechanically that's just **a call box with zero inputs and pull semantics**. Issue 317 is, on a charitable read, introducing this only because today's data boxes don't run code at all — but the right fix may be to retire the distinction rather than teach data boxes a new trick.\n\n**Question back:** do you want data boxes to remain a distinct *kind* (literal-only, no source files), and any source-file-as-value becomes a zero-input call box? That would eliminate 317's spec responsibility entirely.\n\n## 4. Custom per-port translation — is it just swapping a function pointer?\n\nYes, that's exactly right. Today the dispatch path for input `i` is conceptually:\n\n```\nspec_for(box).json_to_native(...)   // or native passthrough\n```\n\nWith per-port custom shims, the per-port slot stores its own `lang_bridge_fn` pointer, and the dispatch path becomes:\n\n```\nport[i].decode(...)   // defaults to the spec's bridge if user hasn't overridden\n```\n\nNo new spec responsibility — it's a dispatch-side indirection. The spec doesn't even need to know whether the bridge that produced its input was the default one or a custom one; it just trusts `input_native[i]`. So I'd revise my earlier answer: **issue 246 is not actually a spec-facing change**, it's a slot/port-table change.\n\n## 5. Encapsulated map as a \"language\"\n\nThink of it this way. Today the dispatch layer has rules like:\n\n- If `box.lang == \"lua\"`, look up the Lua spec, call it.\n- If `box.lang == \"c\"`, look up the C spec, call it.\n- If `box.lang == \"map\"`, ??? (currently special-cased: open the sub-map, walk it, splice outputs).\n\nIssue 248 says: **stop special-casing maps**. Write a `langs/map/spec.so` that satisfies the same `lang_spec_t` contract every other language does. Its `init` loads sub-maps; its `invoke` runs the sub-graph and returns the outputs in the same packed-bytes form a Lua function would. From the dispatch layer's point of view, a `\"map\"` box is now indistinguishable from a `\"lua\"` box.\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- This is the \"language spec is the abstraction boundary\" point made flesh. Once a *graph* satisfies the language-spec interface, the dispatch layer doesn't know — and doesn't need to know — whether it's executing source code or a sub-graph. Both are just \"something callable that conforms.\"\n- The same pattern would let you eventually add `lang: \"remote\"` (call across the network), `lang: \"shell\"` (literal bash one-liner stored inline), `lang: \"constant\"` (which is basically what a literal data box already is). At that point, the spec contract becomes the *only* extension point in the system.\n─────────────────────────────────────────────────\n\n**Question back:** if maps-as-specs goes through, does it tempt you to also retire the box `lang` field entirely and just have a `spec` field that points at the loaded `lang_spec_t`? It would make the encapsulation truly flat."

Let me play it\n  back:\n\n  ▎ \"Compile the pointed-at function and its dependencies at map compile time. Then ask each\n  ▎ language spec for a recipe: how does your language shell out to a binary with arguments?\n  ▎ Generate a wrapper from that recipe.\"\n\n  That's a generate_foreign_call_wrapper(target_lang, fn_signature) responsibility — each spec\n  knows how it shells out (Lua: io.popen, C: execve, Bash: literal subshell), and the compiler\n  stamps wrappers at boundary crossings. The wire just carries the path to the compiled binary\n  plus arguments.\n\n  Issue 318 currently says the spec emits a $function_pointer sentinel and the consumer\n  reconstructs it. That's much vaguer — it doesn't say how the consumer turns the sentinel back\n  into something callable.\n\n  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────\n  - Your design moves the work to compile time and makes it visible (a wrapper is a real file you\n  can read); the sentinel design defers it to runtime and hides it in the bridge. The former is\n  more debuggable and aligns with the \"specs are translators\" framing already emerging.\n  - It also means a function-pointer crossing isn't really \"cross-language\" at the data layer at\n  all — it's two same-language calls with a process boundary in the middle. That's a strictly\n  simpler mental model.\n  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────\n\n  Question back: should I open an amendment to 318 that replaces $function_pointer\n  sentinel-passing with the compile-time wrapper-generation approach? Want me to write that up so\n  you can review?\n\n\nshelling out to a binary with arguments is the only real path forward I can see. If we rely on language specific integrations like luaJIT's FFI interface, then... it's not only going to be brittle, but also it'll become very specific to languages that use those. We want to be agnostic if we can. So, yes, I think the design I just described is better. Unless you can think of any arguments against?\n\n  Question back: if maps-as-specs goes through, does it tempt you to also retire the box lang\n  field entirely and just have a spec field that points at the loaded lang_spec_t? It would make\n  the encapsulation truly flat.\n\nyeah actually I like having pointers for that sort of thing.",
    "role":"user"
  },

## [37]

```json
{
  "timestamp":"2026-05-22T19:20:59.628Z",
  "entrypoint":"cli",
  "gitBranch":"master",
  "version":"2.1.121",
  "uuid":"4c186772-3903-4935-8b09-6fffdb07c81f",
  "parentUuid":"391dca36-c416-4740-b255-b8c5c11372e4",
  "sessionId":"8ec29876-d560-4fbc-934b-227cde0b3a68",
  "userType":"external",
  "isSidechain":false,
  "type":"assistant",
  "requestId":"req_011CbJCWYeuMkkob6K8fjHPs",
  "cwd":"/mnt/mtwo/programs/sora/soramech",
  "message":{
    "content":[{
        "type":"text",
        "text":"Three design calls registered. Two clean confirmations and one that has a real wrinkle worth thinking through.\n\n## 1. Single `invoke`, dispatch decodes JSON-to-native at the ring\n\nYour design is cleaner than what's in the header today. Let me play it back to make sure I have it:\n\n- Each input slot has (conceptually) a native ring and a JSON ring fed by upstream.\n- Dispatch pops the next-most-recent value from whichever ring it came from.\n- If it came from the JSON ring, dispatch calls the consuming spec's `json_to_native` *before* invoke, pushing the value onto the spec's runtime store.\n- By the time `invoke` runs, every argument is native. One entry point, no flags.\n\nThat works. One thing worth naming for symmetry: **outputs still need bridging** when fan-out is cross-language. If a Lua box feeds one Lua consumer and one C consumer, the Lua box produces native Lua once; dispatch calls `native_to_json` once and pushes the JSON onto the C-side ring. That's still bridge work, but it's symmetric with the input path and not \"another invoke variant.\" So you end up with: one `invoke`, plus the two bridges. The three-variant invoke goes away entirely.\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- Your design also makes the spec **simpler to write** — the spec author never has to reason about \"what if some of my inputs are JSON?\" That branching moves into the dispatch layer where it belongs (dispatch already knows the wire format because it just popped the value).\n- The \"lag\" you describe is real but invariant — same total decoding work, just done eagerly at pop time instead of lazily inside invoke. The only difference is *who* drives it. Putting the driver in dispatch keeps specs honest: they speak one language, native.\n- There's a nice second-order effect: this design means `json_to_native` is called *exactly* at language boundaries (where a JSON-ring value enters a native runtime), which is the only place it should ever be called. Today's contract permits specs to internally decide whether to decode, which is one degree of freedom too many.\n─────────────────────────────────────────────────\n\nI'll plan to amend issue 312 along these lines once you confirm. Sound right?\n\n## 2. Shell-out wrappers — yes, with one wrinkle: persistent subprocesses\n\nYou asked for arguments against. I tried to think of real ones, not strawmen:\n\n- **Fork+exec cost is ~1ms per call.** For a tick-driven dataflow loop where cross-language calls happen per tick, that dominates everything. FFI is microseconds. This is the main reason teams reach for FFI even though it's brittle.\n- **State doesn't survive across calls.** A function pointer that holds a counter, a loaded model, a cached connection — all gone the moment the subprocess exits. The wrapper sees a fresh binary every call.\n- **Argument enumeration is hard in dynamic languages.** Lua closures capture upvalues and `_ENV`. Statically figuring out *all the dependencies* of a function pointer at compile time is the FFI problem in different clothes.\n\nBut — and this is the part that resolves it for me — the **threading roadmap already commits to Unix domain sockets for cross-language IPC** (per memory). So the actual implementation isn't \"fork+exec per call.\" It's:\n\n- At map compile time, generate the wrapper *and* spawn the target-language subprocess once. It listens on a Unix socket.\n- The wrapper, when called, sends `(fn_name, args)` over the socket and waits for the result.\n- State lives in the long-running subprocess. Latency is socket round-trip, ~10–50µs, not fork latency.\n\nThat sidesteps the cost concern *and* the state concern in one move. The subprocess becomes the natural home for the function pointer's closure state.\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- The shell-out-binary-with-args framing and the persistent-socket-subprocess framing aren't actually different designs — the first is the simple-case skin of the second. You start with `execve(binary, args)`, and as soon as you want repeat calls to cheap, you keep the binary running and talk via socket. Both still respect the agnostic principle: every language can speak a socket protocol, no language-specific FFI required.\n- This also means the language-spec contract gains *exactly one new recipe* per language: \"how do you generate a wrapper that does a socket round-trip and decodes the reply?\" That's a small, well-bounded responsibility, much more so than \"implement a sentinel reconstruction path.\"\n- Argument enumeration (the upvalue problem) becomes the user's job at map-compile time, which is honest: if you're putting a function pointer on a wire, you owe the system a sufficient description of what it closes over. The map compiler can refuse to compile pointers it can't enumerate, which is a clear, debuggable failure mode.\n─────────────────────────────────────────────────\n\nSo: no real arguments against your design, provided we plan the subprocess-and-socket path from the start rather than ship `execve`-per-call and discover the latency cliff later.\n\n**Question back:** when I rewrite 318, should I scope it to \"function pointer crossings via long-lived subprocess + socket, wrapper generated by spec,\" and explicitly cite the threading roadmap as the IPC mechanism? Or do you want a smaller first cut that does `execve` per call and earns the socket later?\n\n## 3. Spec field is a pointer\n\nNoted. So the box record will eventually carry `lang_spec_t *spec` instead of `const char *lang`. The string lookup happens once, at graph-load, when the loader resolves the name to the dlopen'd module — and from then on it's pointer-equality and direct dispatch.\n\nThat also means **encapsulated-map boxes, function-pointer-wrapper boxes, and source-code boxes all carry the same field shape** — just pointed at different specs. That's the \"flat encapsulation\" payoff: the dispatch layer's case analysis on `box->lang == \"...\"` disappears, replaced by an indirect call through a `lang_spec_t *`. The dispatch loop becomes about a dozen lines."
      }],

## [43]

```json
{
  "timestamp":"2026-05-22T19:29:47.073Z",
  "entrypoint":"cli",
  "gitBranch":"master",

  "uuid":"51558c79-b3d1-4966-ae39-744970595dcc",
  "parentUuid":"6c6beef5-1600-4261-94f2-b264acc41a8e",
  "sessionId":"8ec29876-d560-4fbc-934b-227cde0b3a68",
  "userType":"external",
  "isSidechain":false,

  "message":{
    "content":[{
        "type":"text",
        "text":"Doing the pre-flight. There are two real concerns that need a decision *before* you write code, and a few smaller ones that are fine to handle as they come up.\n\n## Must decide before implementing\n\n### Concern A — Where do decoded native values *live*?\n\nThe current `invoke` signature takes `input_data[]` as bytes-and-sizes. But your new design says dispatch decodes JSON to native *before* invoke. So what does invoke receive?\n\nTwo coherent answers:\n\n1. **Native lives in the runtime store.** Dispatch calls `json_to_native(handle, json_bytes)` which pushes onto Lua's stack (or C's typed-pointer table, etc.). `invoke` takes only `n_inputs` and pulls from the store directly. The bytes arrays in the current signature go away.\n2. **Native is a separate byte form.** Dispatch decodes JSON-bytes → native-bytes (e.g. msgpack for Lua). `invoke` receives those native bytes and decodes them into runtime values itself.\n\n(1) is faster and aligns with the existing bridge convention (Lua's bridge already pushes/pops via stack). (2) keeps the current invoke signature but means every spec maintains *two* serialised forms — a native one and JSON — which is awkward.\n\nI recommend (1). But it means **the invoke signature changes shape** — that's a contract change, not just adding/removing fast paths. Worth being explicit about in the issue.\n\n### Concern B — What does \"same-language wire\" actually mean?\n\nTwo same-language boxes (Lua → Lua) don't share a `lua_State` (each worker has its own; boxes don't generally share state). So the value can't just sit on a stack across the boundary. *Something* has to serialise it.\n\nTwo options:\n\n1. **Same-language wires use language-native bytes (msgpack for Lua, struct memcpy for C).** That needs **two more bridge functions per spec**: `native_to_bytes` and `bytes_to_native`. Cheap, agnostic, but adds to the contract.\n2. **Same-language wires force producer and consumer onto the same worker, share the stack directly.** Much faster (no serialisation at all) but it constrains the scheduler — you've now coupled wire topology to worker affinity. Once a wire goes cross-language, that constraint disappears, so it's a heterogeneous policy.\n\nIf you want the fast path to be genuinely fast, you probably want (2) for same-language and (1) only as the fallback when affinity can't be honoured. But (2) is the bigger commitment — it touches the scheduler, not just the spec contract.\n\nWorth declaring which one you're committing to in 312, or marking the same-language fast path as \"deferred decision; route through JSON for now.\"\n\n## Smaller concerns — flagging, not blocking\n\n- **Bridge contract becomes mandatory, not optional.** Existing specs (Lua, C, Bash) all need bridges before 312 lands or the whole runtime breaks. Worth a migration note.\n- **`invoke_native` / `invoke_json` removal touches the existing header comments and `langs/{lua,c,bash}/spec.c` files** — small mechanical sweep, easy to miss.\n- **Error semantics shift slightly**: today a malformed JSON input is the spec's problem; under the new flow, `json_to_native` raises at the dispatch layer before invoke ever runs. That's actually *better* (errors point at the bridge, not the function) but worth calling out so the dispatch error path knows to report \"input N of box X failed json_to_native\" rather than \"invoke returned nonzero.\"\n- **`box_t *box` parameter to invoke** — keep it. Specs still need it for sentinel emission policy, declared return type, compile hints.\n- **Output fan-out**: when a Lua box has both Lua and C consumers, dispatch needs to convert the output *twice* — once via `native_to_bytes` (or stack-share) for Lua consumers, once via `native_to_json` for the C consumer. Same value, two routes. The bridge contract handles it but the dispatch code needs to remember to call both, not just one.\n\n`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`\n- The contract is migrating from \"one entry per format\" to \"one entry, plus four directional bridges.\" Symmetric and predictable: `{json, native_bytes} × {to_runtime, from_runtime}`. That's a coordinate system, not a list of cases — much easier to reason about as the system grows.\n- Concern B is the one that has long-term scheduling implications. A → B → C where A,B are Lua and C is C: do A and B *have* to be on the same worker? If yes, you've made worker assignment a graph-coloring problem. If no, you've given up the stack-sharing fast path. Worth committing to one answer in 312, even if the commitment is \"defer — route through JSON for v1.\"\n─────────────────────────────────────────────────\n\n## My recommendation for 312 scope\n\n- Resolve Concern A as design (1): native lives in the runtime store; invoke loses its bytes arrays.\n- Resolve Concern B by **explicitly deferring** the worker-affinity question and routing same-language wires through `native_to_bytes` / `bytes_to_native` (msgpack-style) for v1. The faster stack-sharing path becomes a future issue.\n- Make all four bridges (`json_to_native`, `native_to_json`, `bytes_to_native`, `native_to_bytes`) required, not optional.\n- Delete `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` from the contract.\n\nSound like the right scope? If yes, I'll start the implementation now."
      }],
