```json
    "content":"  1. Native lives in the runtime store. Dispatch calls json_to_native(handle, json_bytes) which\n  pushes onto Lua's stack (or C's typed-pointer table, etc.). invoke takes only n_inputs and pulls\n   from the store directly. The bytes arrays in the current signature go away.\n\n\n  Concern B — What does \"same-language wire\" actually mean?\n\n  Two same-language boxes (Lua → Lua) don't share a lua_State (each worker has its own; boxes\n  don't generally share state). So the value can't just sit on a stack across the boundary.\n  Something has to serialise it.\n\n\nthe values are stored in shared memory. This question is about native->native specifically so I won't discuss json here - the values are written, in the format that lua expects, to a piece of shared memory by the producer thread. This piece is the slot allocated for the box's native ring buffer. A pointer to that slot is added to the \"FIFO notebook\" in the ring buffer manager. Then, when the consumer thread reads from the the ring buffer, the value is pushed to the lua stack, or however else the language is specced to consume values. In native->native, they are stored as bytes, exactly as the language spec expects them, because that's the way they were emitted. I don't think this needs extra bridge functions - it should be part of the functions that write return values to input slot ring buffers. There's no such thing as \"bytes vs native\" - there is only native.\n\nplease don't do any implementation right now. Just update the issue files for all the issues we discussed. I think it was 312 and 318 primarily...?",

  },
```

```json
{
  "sessionId":"8ec29876-d560-4fbc-934b-227cde0b3a68",
```

```json
        "type":"tool_result",
        "content":"1\t# 312 — Same-language wire fast path: dual-ring slot, JSON at language borders\n2\t\n3\t## Status\n4\tsubstantively complete (2026-05-21) — dual-ring slots, per-edge\n5\tclassification, spec invoke signature with per-input flags, Lua\n6\tinput-side branching, and dispatch consultation all landed. The\n7\tdeferred output-side coordinated format (cross-language JSON\n8\temission from Lua / C / Bash) lives in issues 307 and 308; this\n9\tissue's design covers it but the implementation crosses into\n10\teach spec's per-language work.\n11\t\n12\t## Current behavior\n13\t\n14\tToday every wire's bytes are whatever the producing spec wrote, and\n15\tthe consuming spec reads them verbatim. The Lua spec emits JSON via\n16\t`lua_native_to_json` (`langs/lua/spec.c:335`); the C and Bash specs\n17\thave no bridge implementations yet and only carry whatever bytes\n18\ttheir bytes-in-bytes-out `invoke` produced.\n19\t\n20\tThe dispatch layer picks the spec callback (`invoke_native` /\n21\t`invoke_json` / fallback `invoke`) based on a single per-box\n22\t`use_native_invoke` bit computed at graph load — true only when\n23\t**every** input edge and **every** output edge of the box stays\n24\twithin one language. That's the pessimistic per-box approximation:\n25\ta box whose output fans to one same-language consumer and one\n26\tcross-language consumer is classified as cross-language for the\n27\twhole box, so the same-language neighbor eats the JSON cost too.\n28\t\n29\tThe wire-format design question — what shape the slot has when\n30\tsame-language hops should stay native and JSON should only show up\n31\tat language borders — has not been settled in code. This issue\n32\tsettles it.\n33\t\n34\t## Intended behavior — dual-ring slot\n35\t\n36\t**Each input slot is two ring buffers plus an ordering ring.**\n37\t\n38\t```\n39\tslot {\n40\t    ring_native  : ring of cells holding consumer-lang's native bytes\n41\t    ring_json    : ring of cells holding JSON bytes (cross-language values)\n42\t    ring_order   : ring of (which_ring, idx) tuples in arrival order\n43\t    ... existing peek / pop modes apply to ring_order ...\n44\t}\n45\t```\n46\t\n47\t**Producer push** (per outgoing connection):\n48\t\n49\t- If the connection's per-edge bit `output_edge_native[j]` is true,\n50\t  the producer's spec writes its native bytes to the consumer\n51\t  slot's `ring_native`.\n52\t- Otherwise the spec converts to JSON via `native_to_json` and\n53\t  writes to `ring_json`.\n54\t- Either way, push a `(which_ring, idx)` tuple to `ring_order`.\n55\t\n56\t**Consumer pop**:\n57\t\n58\t- Pop `ring_order` to learn which ring the next value lives in.\n59\t- Pop the cell at the indicated index in the indicated ring.\n60\t- The consumer's spec receives a `(native | json, bytes)` pair per\n61\t  input and picks the matching decoder.\n62\t\n63\tThis shape is precise on **mixed fan-in ports**: a port fed by one\n64\tLua producer and one C producer keeps the Lua values native in\n65\t`ring_native` and the C-translated-to-JSON values in `ring_json`.\n66\tThe consumer pays JSON-decode cost only on cross-language cells,\n67\tnot on the entire port. The per-port-classification alternative\n68\t(slot is all-JSON if any fan-in is cross-lang) is the simpler\n69\tfallback — strictly correct, but forces native producers to eat\n70\tJSON on mixed-fan-in ports. Dual-ring keeps each cell at the\n71\tright cost.\n72\t\n73\t### Per-language native bytes\n74\t\n75\tEach spec picks its own native byte format. The only contract is\n76\tinternal symmetry: the spec's own native-write and native-read have\n77\tto round-trip its own runtime values.\n78\t\n79\t- **Lua** — bytes the spec can deserialize back to a Lua value\n80\t  quickly. Candidates: msgpack, `lua_dump` for closures, a custom\n81\t  binary form. `cjson.encode` is a valid starting point (a JSON\n82\t  string the Lua spec parses with `cjson.decode`); refine later if\n83\t  profiling shows the encode/decode is hot.\n84\t- **C** — `memcpy` of the typed struct. The C spec's generated\n85\t  wrapper (issue 307) knows the layout, so the ring cell holds the\n86\t  struct's bytes verbatim.\n87\t- **Bash** — strings. Bash has no other native form; its native\n88\t  ring stores string bytes directly.\n89\t\n90\t### Why JSON for the cross-language ring\n91\t\n92\tJSON's structure is self-describing — six kinds (object, array,\n93\tnumber, string, bool, null), each with an obvious mapping into\n94\tevery language's runtime. Adding a new language K means K's spec\n95\timplements one pair of bridges: `native_to_json` and\n96\t`json_to_native`. No spec ever needs code that knows another\n97\tlanguage's types. The N-language matrix collapses to N spec-pair\n98\timplementations, not N² pair-wise paths.\n99\t\n100\tThe harder cases — closures, opaque language values, function\n101\tpointers — don't fit JSON's structure. JSON carries them via\n102\tsentinel primitives:\n103\t\n104\t- `$ref` — pointer + length + type tag for bytes in the large-value\n105\t  heap. In-process specs deref directly; cross-process boundaries\n106\t  inline the bytes at the UDS marshaller.\n107\t- `$function_pointer` — in-process address + signature for\n108\t  callables. Wire compile fails if the consumer's language can't\n109\t  reconstruct.\n110\t- `$lang_opaque` — \"these bytes are language-X-internal.\" The\n111\t  destination spec either knows what to do or wire compile fails.\n112\t\n113\tFirst-cut behavior: `encode_value` in the Lua spec hard-errors on\n114\tfunction / userdata / thread (`langs/lua/spec.c:317`). Sentinels\n115\tland in a later slice.\n116\t\n117\t## Per-edge classification at graph load\n118\t\n119\tThe graph loader's single-threaded phase walks every connection\n120\tand computes per-box arrays:\n121\t\n122\t- `input_edge_native[i]` — one bit per input port. True iff every\n123\t  producer feeding that port shares the consumer's language.\n124\t- `output_edge_native[j]` — one bit per outgoing connection. True\n125\t  iff the consumer on the other end shares this box's language.\n126\t\n127\tBoth arrays are already declared and populated — see\n128\t`src/010-graph-loader.h:145` and `src/010-graph-loader.c:689-754`.\n129\t\n130\tUnder dual-ring, only `output_edge_native[j]` is consulted on the\n131\tpush path (it picks the ring at write time). The consumer-side bit\n132\t`input_edge_native[i]` is now informational — the actual per-cell\n133\tring tag lives in `ring_order` and is precise per cell.\n134\t\n135\tThe per-box `use_native_invoke` summary is **removed**. The\n136\tdispatch reads `ring_order` per pop and consults the cell's tag\n137\tdirectly.\n138\t\n139\t### Why per-edge, not per-box\n140\t\n141\tThe earlier per-box approximation collapsed on the first\n142\tcross-language edge it found — a Lua box with one C neighbor went\n143\tJSON across all its other edges. Per-edge keeps the cost local to\n144\tthe actual cross-language boundary. The interior of any\n145\tsame-language island skips JSON; only the boundary edges pay. The\n146\timplementation cost is identical: same number of bits per box,\n147\tstored per-port / per-connection instead of per-box.\n148\t\n149\tDual-ring takes precision one step further: even on a single port\n150\twith mixed fan-in, each individual cell is in the right ring. The\n151\tboundary tightens from \"edges that cross languages\" to \"cells\n152\tthat crossed languages.\"\n153\t\n154\t## Runtime dispatch\n155\t\n156\t### Input-read\n157\t\n158\tFor each input port:\n159\t```c\n160\t(which_ring, idx) = ring_order_pop(slot->ring_order)\n161\tcell_bytes        = ring_at(which_ring == NATIVE ? slot->ring_native\n162\t                                                  : slot->ring_json,\n163\t                            idx)\n164\tinput_data[i]   = cell_bytes\n165\tinput_native[i] = (which_ring == NATIVE)\n166\t```\n167\t\n168\t`input_native[i]` is a per-input flag the spec consults to know\n169\twhether `input_data[i]` is its native form or JSON.\n170\t\n171\t### Spec invoke\n172\t\n173\tThe spec's `invoke` signature gains per-input and per-output flags:\n174\t\n175\t```c\n176\tint invoke(handle, file_path, fn_name,\n177\t           const void **input_data, const int *input_sizes,\n178\t           const int *input_native, int n_inputs,\n179\t           int output_native,\n180\t           void *out_buf, int out_buf_capacity, int *out_size);\n181\t```\n182\t\n183\tThe spec dispatches per input: if `input_native[i]`, decode as\n184\tnative; else decode as JSON. The output is written in whatever\n185\tform `output_native` indicates — one form, one write. The previously\n186\tseparate `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` callbacks collapse into\n187\tthis single dispatching `invoke`; the per-input dispatch is what\n188\tthe design actually needs.\n189\t\n190\tFor boxes whose output fans to multiple consumers in different\n191\tlanguages, the dispatch picks one form for `output_native` based\n192\ton whichever ring is dominant (typically native if any\n193\tsame-language consumer exists; the cross-language consumers eat a\n194\t`native_to_json` conversion at push time). The detail is a runtime\n195\toptimization, not a correctness concern.\n196\t\n197\t### Output-push\n198\t\n199\t```c\n200\tfor each outgoing connection j:\n201\t    if output_edge_native[j]:\n202\t        push native_bytes      to consumer->ring_native\n203\t        push (NATIVE, idx)     to consumer->ring_order\n204\t    else:\n205\t        if output_was_native: json_bytes = spec->native_to_json(native_bytes)\n206\t        push json_bytes        to consumer->ring_json\n207\t        push (JSON, idx)       to consumer->ring_order\n208\t```\n209\t\n210\tIf the spec produced native and multiple cross-language consumers\n211\texist, the dispatch calls `native_to_json` once and reuses the\n212\tJSON bytes across every cross-language push (optimization, not\n213\trequired for correctness).\n214\t\n215\t## Edge cases\n216\t\n217\t- **All-same-language graph**: every connection's\n218\t  `output_edge_native[j]` is true; `ring_json` stays unallocated\n219\t  across every slot; activity is on `ring_native` and `ring_order`\n220\t  only.\n221\t- **All-different-language graph**: every connection routes to\n222\t  `ring_json`; `ring_native` stays unallocated.\n223\t- **Mixed fan-in**: both rings active on the same slot. Consumer\n224\t  sees a mix of native and JSON cells, picked apart by\n225\t  `ring_order`.\n226\t- **Type changes mid-development**: a wire that was Lua→Lua\n227\t  becomes Lua→C when the user changes a downstream box's language.\n228\t  The graph loader recomputes the per-edge bits on the next load;\n229\t  no data conversion at runtime, no migration.\n230\t\n231\t## Implementation cost\n232\t\n233\t- **Slot store**: two rings per slot instead of one, plus the\n234\t  ordering ring. Allocate `ring_json` and `ring_order` lazily —\n235\t  same-language-only slots pay nothing for them. Per-cell cost:\n236\t  one extra ordering-ring write on push, one extra read on pop.\n237\t- **Spec interface**: one signature change to the `invoke`\n238\t  callback adding `input_native[]` and `output_native`. Every\n239\t  spec's `invoke` gains a per-input branch. Lua's branch is\n240\t  almost free — both decoders (`json_to_native` and the future\n241\t  native one) already exist as bridges. C and Bash need their\n242\t  JSON bridges written, which is shared with the broader\n243\t  cross-language work in 307/308.\n244\t- **Dispatch**: one extra step on read (consult ordering ring),\n245\t  one extra branch on push (which ring to write).\n246\t\n247\tThe win is exactness: same-language values stay native even when\n248\ttheir port has a cross-language sibling producer.\n249\t\n250\t## Future: whole-program same-language compilation (issue 313)\n251\t\n252\t313 describes merging all same-language source into one\n253\ttranslation unit per language at compile time. The merge does\n254\t**not** eliminate the slot store for same-language wires — boxes\n255\tstill run as separate tasks in the thread pool, possibly on\n256\tdifferent workers, so values still need a place to wait for\n257\tsibling inputs to arrive. The thread pool's parallelism depends\n258\ton tasks being scheduled independently; collapsing same-language\n259\thops to direct function calls would serialize execution and defeat\n260\tthe pool.\n261\t\n262\tWhat 313 thins is the **spec invocation overhead**: for boxes in\n263\tthe merged region, the dispatch can call the function directly on\n264\tthe worker's `lua_State` (or compiled C code) without going\n265\tthrough dlsym + bytes-marshal + bridge. The dual-ring slot shape\n266\tremains; 313 makes the native-ring's read/write cheaper. The two\n267\tlayers stack, they don't cancel.\n268\t\n269\t## Resolved design choice: JSON, not type-annotated raw bytes\n270\t\n271\tAn earlier note asked whether cross-language wires should use\n272\ttype-annotated raw byte encodings (length-prefixed ints, raw\n273\tfloats, etc.) instead of JSON. **Decided: JSON across the board\n274\tfor the cross-language ring.** Rationale:\n275\t\n276\t- JSON's self-describing structure makes a new language one\n277\t  spec-pair of `native_to_json` / `json_to_native` — no per-pair\n278\t  language paths.\n279\t- The native ring's format is each spec's private choice, so the\n280\t  per-language performance flexibility is there without universal\n281\t  type-tag complexity.\n282\t- Sentinel primitives in JSON (`$ref`, `$function_pointer`,\n283\t  `$lang_opaque`) carry the structurally awkward cases without\n284\t  forcing the wire format to grow new top-level shapes.\n285\t\n286\t## Suggested implementation sequence\n287\t\n288\t1. **Extend the slot data structure**. Add `ring_native`,\n289\t   `ring_json`, `ring_order` to the slot type in\n290\t   `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}`. Allocate `ring_json` and\n291\t   `ring_order` lazily.\n292\t2. **Extend the spec `invoke` signature**. Update `lang_invoke_fn`\n293\t   in `langs/lang-spec.h` to add `const int *input_native`,\n294\t   `int output_native`. Touch every spec's `invoke` stub to accept\n295\t   (and initially ignore) the new parameters.\n296\t3. **Update dispatch input-read** (`src/012-dispatch.c`). Pop\n297\t   `ring_order` first, then the named ring. Populate\n298\t   `input_native[]` per input port.\n299\t4. **Update dispatch output-push** (`src/012-dispatch.c`). Select\n300\t   the consumer's ring based on `output_edge_native[j]`. Write\n301\t   the ordering-ring tuple. Reuse JSON across cross-language\n302\t   consumers when the producer fanned to multiple.\n303\t5. **Lua spec** (`langs/lua/spec.c`). Branch per `input_native[i]`\n304\t   — decode native or JSON accordingly. Pick the native byte\n305\t   format (start with `cjson.encode`; refine later).\n306\t6. **C and Bash spec bridges**. Implement `native_to_json` and\n307\t   `json_to_native` for C and Bash so cross-language pushes work.\n308\t   C's native form is `memcpy` of the wrapper's declared struct;\n309\t   Bash's native form is plain strings.\n310\t7. **Remove `use_native_invoke`**. Drop the per-box field from\n311\t   `box_t` and its computation from `graph_load`. The dispatch\n312\t   consults the ordering ring's per-cell tag, not a per-box\n313\t   summary.\n314\t8. **Tests**. A 100k-iteration Lua→Lua pipeline uses `ring_native`\n315\t   exclusively. A mixed-lang pipeline exercises both rings. A\n316\t   mixed-fan-in port shows both rings active on one slot.\n317\t\n318\t## Relevant files\n319\t\n320\t- `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}` — slot data structure; the\n321\t  dual-ring extension lands here.\n322\t- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — per-edge classification (already\n323\t  done at `010-graph-loader.c:689-754`).\n324\t- `src/012-dispatch.{c,h}` — input-read, output-push.\n325\t- `langs/lang-spec.h` — `invoke` signature extension.\n326\t- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c` — per-spec\n327\t  implementations.\n328\t- `issues/302-wire-value-slot-store.md` — original single-ring\n329\t  slot design (completed); the dual-ring extension is the structural\n330\t  amendment captured here.\n331\t- `issues/306-lua-language-spec.md`, `307-c-language-spec.md`,\n332\t  `308-bash-language-spec.md` — per-spec typed marshalling lands on\n333\t  top of this issue.\n334\t- `issues/313-research-whole-program-same-language-merge.md` —\n335\t  follow-on that thins spec invocation for merged regions.\n336\t- `issues/245-wire-color-by-cross-language-classification.md` —\n337\t  editor-side visual reflection of the per-edge classification\n338\t  (green for JSON, gray for native).\n339\t\n340\t## Implementation status\n341\t\n342\tWhat's in place:\n343\t\n344\t- `lang_spec_t` carries `invoke_native`, `invoke_json`,\n345\t  `native_to_json`, `json_to_native` slots (current shape — to be\n346\t  collapsed to a single `invoke` with per-input/per-output flags\n347\t  per this issue).\n348\t- Lua's `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are real\n349\t  (`langs/lua/spec.c:335` and `:437`).\n350\t- `input_edge_native[]` and `output_edge_native[]` are computed at\n351\t  graph load (`src/010-graph-loader.c:689-754`).\n352\t- Per-box `use_native_invoke` summary is computed and consulted by\n353\t  dispatch — to be removed.\n354\t- **Slice 1 complete (2026-05-21).** Dual-ring slot data structure\n355\t  in `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}`: `SLOT_FLAG_DUAL_RING`,\n356\t  `SLOT_RING_NATIVE` / `SLOT_RING_JSON` constants, three internal\n357\t  rings per slot (native data, JSON data, ordering) under one\n358\t  per-slot spinlock, new API `slot_push_native`, `slot_push_json`,\n359\t  `slot_pop_ordered`. Single per-slot lock makes the data+ordering\n360\t  write atomic so consumers see push pairs intact. On non-dual\n361\t  slots `slot_push_native` and `slot_pop_ordered` alias to the\n362\t  existing `slot_push` / `slot_pop`; `slot_push_json` returns -1.\n363\t  On dual slots the old single-ring API returns -1 (caller must\n364\t  pick a ring). `slot_has_value` and `slot_fill_count` consult\n365\t  the ordering ring on dual slots so spawn-on-input-ready and\n366\t  test introspection still work. Five new tests in\n367\t  `tests/009-slot-store-test.c` cover the happy paths\n368\t  (native-only, mixed arrival order, wraparound), the\n369\t  order-ring-as-binding-capacity-constraint, and the back-compat\n370\t  cases.\n371\t\n372\t- **Slice 2 complete (2026-05-21).** `lang_invoke_fn` typedef in\n373\t  `langs/lang-spec.h` gained `const int *input_native` (between\n374\t  `input_sizes` and `n_inputs`) and `int output_native` (after\n375\t  `n_inputs`, before `out_buf`). Because all three callback slots\n376\t  (`invoke`, `invoke_native`, `invoke_json`) share the same\n377\t  typedef, the change propagates automatically. Every spec's\n378\t  invoke function picked up the new params and currently ignores\n379\t  them — the per-input branching is slice 4 (Lua) and 307 / 308\n380\t  (C and Bash). Dispatch's `do_call_box` passes `NULL` /\n381\t  `0` placeholder values; slice 3 wires up the real per-cell\n382\t  flags from the dual-ring's ordering pop. Eleven direct\n383\t  call sites in the spec test files updated to the new signature.\n384\t- **Slice 3 complete (2026-05-21).** Call-box input ports become\n385\t  dual-ring slots (in `graph_attach_runtime`) when their shape\n386\t  permits — restrictions inherited from slice 1: not LARGE_VALUE,\n387\t  not TAGGED. Dispatch's `read_inputs` consults\n388\t  `slot_flags(slot_id)` and routes dual-ring slots through\n389\t  `slot_pop_ordered`, populating `input_native[i]` from the\n390\t  per-cell `which_ring` tag returned by the pop; single-ring\n391\t  slots keep the existing `slot_peek` / `slot_pop` path and\n392\t  derive `input_native[i]` from the per-port\n393\t  `input_edge_native[i]` classification. Push side\n394\t  (`push_one_connection`) now takes the producer's connection\n395\t  index, branches on the consumer slot's flags, and picks\n396\t  `slot_push_native` vs `slot_push_json` based on the producer's\n397\t  `output_edge_native[j]`. Literal pushes from\n398\t  `dispatch_push_literals` go through `slot_push_native` (literals\n399\t  are configured in the consumer's own box JSON, so semantically\n400\t  same-language). `do_write_box`'s `read_inputs` call passes\n401\t  `NULL` for the flag array since write boxes aren't spec-invoked.\n402\t  Added `slot_flags(s, id)` accessor to the slot store API so\n403\t  dispatch can branch without storing per-port bookkeeping.\n404\t  Existing tests stay green (22 slot-store, 14 graph-loader,\n405\t  7 dispatch, 11 integration including multi-language `pipeline`\n406\t  and `driver-test`); test fixtures that pushed directly via\n407\t  `slot_push` on what's now a dual-ring slot were migrated to\n408\t  `slot_push_native`.\n409\t\n410\t- **Slice 4 complete (2026-05-21).** Lua spec's `lua_invoke`\n411\t  branches per `input_native[i]`: NATIVE (or NULL slice-2 callers)\n412\t  push via `lua_pushlstring` exactly as before; JSON\n413\t  (`input_native[i] == 0`) calls a new shared `parse_json_to_stack`\n414\t  helper that lifts the bytes into a Lua value via the existing\n415\t  `decode_node`. On JSON parse failure the spec falls back to\n416\t  `lua_pushlstring` silently — that's the bridge to today's\n417\t  cross-language producers (C / Bash) which haven't started\n418\t  emitting real JSON yet; preserves backward compatibility while\n419\t  the input-format contract tightens. `lua_json_to_native` was\n420\t  refactored to use the same helper, just with `verbose=1` so the\n421\t  bridge path remains loud on parse failures (a cross-language\n422\t  wire that ought to carry JSON but doesn't is a real wire\n423\t  error). Output-side branching on `output_native` deferred —\n424\t  that's a coordinated change with the C and Bash specs and the\n425\t  dispatch's per-call output format computation; see \"what's\n426\t  still ahead\" below. Three tests in `tests/306-lua-spec-test.c`\n427\t  cover the new behavior: `invoke_json_input` (JSON table parses\n428\t  and indexes correctly), `invoke_native_input_with_flag`\n429\t  (NATIVE flag keeps the raw-string path), and\n430\t  `invoke_json_input_falls_back_to_raw` (non-JSON bytes with\n431\t  JSON flag fall back gracefully).\n432\t\n433\t- **Slice 4.5 (2026-05-21) — per-wire writing style + Lua JSON\n434\t  output.** Two coordinated halves:\n435\t  - **Per-wire push.** Each wire keeps its own format. The\n436\t    dispatch picks the consumer slot's native or JSON ring per\n437\t    outgoing connection based on `output_edge_native[j]`. A box\n438\t    with mixed fan-out (some same-lang consumers, some\n439\t    cross-lang) lets each consumer get the right format for its\n440\t    own wire.\n441\t  - **Lua writes JSON when any consumer is cross-language.** The\n442\t    spec passes `output_native = 1` to invoke if every outgoing\n443\t    wire is same-language, else `0`. On the `0` branch the Lua\n444\t    spec serializes the return via the same encoder that powers\n445\t    the `native_to_json` bridge, so cross-language consumers\n446\t    receive parseable JSON (numbers, strings, tables, arrays).\n447\t    On `1` the spec keeps the existing tostring coercion. C-side\n448\t    JSON-input acceptance landed in 307 alongside this, so a Lua\n449\t    → C wire with a primitive payload now round-trips through\n450\t    real JSON in both directions. The pipeline integration test\n451\t    exercises this end-to-end: Lua produces a number, JSON-\n452\t    encodes to bare digits, C unwraps and reads.\n453\t  - **Known limitation for mixed fan-out.** A producer with\n454\t    both same-lang and cross-lang consumers writes one format\n455\t    (JSON, picked by the AND of `output_edge_native[j]`). The\n456\t    same-lang consumers in that case receive JSON bytes through\n457\t    their native ring — Lua's input-side parse-with-fallback\n458\t    absorbs this, so primitives still round-trip. A full per-wire\n459\t    output (producer leaves the value in its runtime; dispatch\n460\t    calls `native_to_json` per cross-lang wire to materialize\n461\t    JSON only where needed) is a deeper contract change tracked\n462\t    as a future slice.\n463\t- **Slice 5 complete (2026-05-21).** The per-box\n464\t  `use_native_invoke` field and its computation are gone. The\n465\t  dispatch's invoke-fn selector collapsed to a single\n466\t  `spec->invoke` call — the per-input and per-output format flags\n467\t  carry the information the old `invoke_native` / `invoke_json`\n468\t  callback split was meant to encode. The spec interface's\n469\t  `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` slots remain as optional\n470\t  no-ops; future specs can specialize on them if profiling\n471\t  justifies, but the dispatch no longer requires them. Three\n472\t  test-side references in `tests/010-graph-loader-test.c` that\n473\t  asserted on the per-box bit were updated to assert on the\n474\t  per-edge bits they were already alongside.\n475\t\n476\tWhat still depends on this issue but lives elsewhere:\n477\t\n478\t- **307 / 308 — C and Bash JSON-input handling**, plus the\n479\t  symmetric output side. Once those land, slice 4.5's deferred\n480\t  Lua-side JSON write can also land and cross-language tables /\n481\t  structured values will round-trip.\n482\t- **318 — sentinel primitives**, the JSON-wire shapes that carry\n483\t  closures / pointers / opaque language values across the\n484\t  cross-language ring.\n485\t\n486\t## Open questions\n487\t\n488\t- **Lua's native byte format**: start with `cjson.encode` (still a\n489\t  win over the json-ring path because the consumer's spec\n490\t  recognises its own format and skips a parse), or jump straight\n491\t  to msgpack? Defer the decision; the dual-ring shape doesn't\n492\t  depend on the choice.\n493\t- **Sharing JSON across multiple cross-language consumers**: the\n494\t  dispatch can call `native_to_json` once and push the same bytes\n495\t  to N cross-language consumer slots. Worthwhile when N > 1; the\n496\t  push path needs a small buffer to hold the JSON across the fan\n497\t  loop. Implement after the basic path works.\n498\t- **Bash native = JSON?**: Bash has no native form distinct from\n499\t  strings, and JSON is already a string. Bash's native ring may\n500\t  hold JSON-shaped strings (effectively aliasing the JSON ring's\n501\t  content). Resolve by inspection once the bash spec writeup\n502\t  starts.\n503\t",

  "uuid":"6b1670ac-a4b2-4cff-b1f2-3dc3abb418f1",
  "parentUuid":"2135d27f-2d71-41bc-a4cf-c580cfb26d0e",
  "toolUseResult":{
    "type":"text",
    "file":{
      "content":"# 312 — Same-language wire fast path: dual-ring slot, JSON at language borders\n\n## Status\nsubstantively complete (2026-05-21) — dual-ring slots, per-edge\nclassification, spec invoke signature with per-input flags, Lua\ninput-side branching, and dispatch consultation all landed. The\ndeferred output-side coordinated format (cross-language JSON\nemission from Lua / C / Bash) lives in issues 307 and 308; this\nissue's design covers it but the implementation crosses into\neach spec's per-language work.\n\n## Current behavior\n\nToday every wire's bytes are whatever the producing spec wrote, and\nthe consuming spec reads them verbatim. The Lua spec emits JSON via\n`lua_native_to_json` (`langs/lua/spec.c:335`); the C and Bash specs\nhave no bridge implementations yet and only carry whatever bytes\ntheir bytes-in-bytes-out `invoke` produced.\n\nThe dispatch layer picks the spec callback (`invoke_native` /\n`invoke_json` / fallback `invoke`) based on a single per-box\n`use_native_invoke` bit computed at graph load — true only when\n**every** input edge and **every** output edge of the box stays\nwithin one language. That's the pessimistic per-box approximation:\na box whose output fans to one same-language consumer and one\ncross-language consumer is classified as cross-language for the\nwhole box, so the same-language neighbor eats the JSON cost too.\n\nThe wire-format design question — what shape the slot has when\nsame-language hops should stay native and JSON should only show up\nat language borders — has not been settled in code. This issue\nsettles it.\n\n## Intended behavior — dual-ring slot\n\n**Each input slot is two ring buffers plus an ordering ring.**\n\n```\nslot {\n    ring_native  : ring of cells holding consumer-lang's native bytes\n    ring_json    : ring of cells holding JSON bytes (cross-language values)\n    ring_order   : ring of (which_ring, idx) tuples in arrival order\n    ... existing peek / pop modes apply to ring_order ...\n}\n```\n\n**Producer push** (per outgoing connection):\n\n- If the connection's per-edge bit `output_edge_native[j]` is true,\n  the producer's spec writes its native bytes to the consumer\n  slot's `ring_native`.\n- Otherwise the spec converts to JSON via `native_to_json` and\n  writes to `ring_json`.\n- Either way, push a `(which_ring, idx)` tuple to `ring_order`.\n\n**Consumer pop**:\n\n- Pop `ring_order` to learn which ring the next value lives in.\n- Pop the cell at the indicated index in the indicated ring.\n- The consumer's spec receives a `(native | json, bytes)` pair per\n  input and picks the matching decoder.\n\nThis shape is precise on **mixed fan-in ports**: a port fed by one\nLua producer and one C producer keeps the Lua values native in\n`ring_native` and the C-translated-to-JSON values in `ring_json`.\nThe consumer pays JSON-decode cost only on cross-language cells,\nnot on the entire port. The per-port-classification alternative\n(slot is all-JSON if any fan-in is cross-lang) is the simpler\nfallback — strictly correct, but forces native producers to eat\nJSON on mixed-fan-in ports. Dual-ring keeps each cell at the\nright cost.\n\n### Per-language native bytes\n\nEach spec picks its own native byte format. The only contract is\ninternal symmetry: the spec's own native-write and native-read have\nto round-trip its own runtime values.\n\n- **Lua** — bytes the spec can deserialize back to a Lua value\n  quickly. Candidates: msgpack, `lua_dump` for closures, a custom\n  binary form. `cjson.encode` is a valid starting point (a JSON\n  string the Lua spec parses with `cjson.decode`); refine later if\n  profiling shows the encode/decode is hot.\n- **C** — `memcpy` of the typed struct. The C spec's generated\n  wrapper (issue 307) knows the layout, so the ring cell holds the\n  struct's bytes verbatim.\n- **Bash** — strings. Bash has no other native form; its native\n  ring stores string bytes directly.\n\n### Why JSON for the cross-language ring\n\nJSON's structure is self-describing — six kinds (object, array,\nnumber, string, bool, null), each with an obvious mapping into\nevery language's runtime. Adding a new language K means K's spec\nimplements one pair of bridges: `native_to_json` and\n`json_to_native`. No spec ever needs code that knows another\nlanguage's types. The N-language matrix collapses to N spec-pair\nimplementations, not N² pair-wise paths.\n\nThe harder cases — closures, opaque language values, function\npointers — don't fit JSON's structure. JSON carries them via\nsentinel primitives:\n\n- `$ref` — pointer + length + type tag for bytes in the large-value\n  heap. In-process specs deref directly; cross-process boundaries\n  inline the bytes at the UDS marshaller.\n- `$function_pointer` — in-process address + signature for\n  callables. Wire compile fails if the consumer's language can't\n  reconstruct.\n- `$lang_opaque` — \"these bytes are language-X-internal.\" The\n  destination spec either knows what to do or wire compile fails.\n\nFirst-cut behavior: `encode_value` in the Lua spec hard-errors on\nfunction / userdata / thread (`langs/lua/spec.c:317`). Sentinels\nland in a later slice.\n\n## Per-edge classification at graph load\n\nThe graph loader's single-threaded phase walks every connection\nand computes per-box arrays:\n\n- `input_edge_native[i]` — one bit per input port. True iff every\n  producer feeding that port shares the consumer's language.\n- `output_edge_native[j]` — one bit per outgoing connection. True\n  iff the consumer on the other end shares this box's language.\n\nBoth arrays are already declared and populated — see\n`src/010-graph-loader.h:145` and `src/010-graph-loader.c:689-754`.\n\nUnder dual-ring, only `output_edge_native[j]` is consulted on the\npush path (it picks the ring at write time). The consumer-side bit\n`input_edge_native[i]` is now informational — the actual per-cell\nring tag lives in `ring_order` and is precise per cell.\n\nThe per-box `use_native_invoke` summary is **removed**. The\ndispatch reads `ring_order` per pop and consults the cell's tag\ndirectly.\n\n### Why per-edge, not per-box\n\nThe earlier per-box approximation collapsed on the first\ncross-language edge it found — a Lua box with one C neighbor went\nJSON across all its other edges. Per-edge keeps the cost local to\nthe actual cross-language boundary. The interior of any\nsame-language island skips JSON; only the boundary edges pay. The\nimplementation cost is identical: same number of bits per box,\nstored per-port / per-connection instead of per-box.\n\nDual-ring takes precision one step further: even on a single port\nwith mixed fan-in, each individual cell is in the right ring. The\nboundary tightens from \"edges that cross languages\" to \"cells\nthat crossed languages.\"\n\n## Runtime dispatch\n\n### Input-read\n\nFor each input port:\n```c\n(which_ring, idx) = ring_order_pop(slot->ring_order)\ncell_bytes        = ring_at(which_ring == NATIVE ? slot->ring_native\n                                                  : slot->ring_json,\n                            idx)\ninput_data[i]   = cell_bytes\ninput_native[i] = (which_ring == NATIVE)\n```\n\n`input_native[i]` is a per-input flag the spec consults to know\nwhether `input_data[i]` is its native form or JSON.\n\n### Spec invoke\n\nThe spec's `invoke` signature gains per-input and per-output flags:\n\n```c\nint invoke(handle, file_path, fn_name,\n           const void **input_data, const int *input_sizes,\n           const int *input_native, int n_inputs,\n           int output_native,\n           void *out_buf, int out_buf_capacity, int *out_size);\n```\n\nThe spec dispatches per input: if `input_native[i]`, decode as\nnative; else decode as JSON. The output is written in whatever\nform `output_native` indicates — one form, one write. The previously\nseparate `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` callbacks collapse into\nthis single dispatching `invoke`; the per-input dispatch is what\nthe design actually needs.\n\nFor boxes whose output fans to multiple consumers in different\nlanguages, the dispatch picks one form for `output_native` based\non whichever ring is dominant (typically native if any\nsame-language consumer exists; the cross-language consumers eat a\n`native_to_json` conversion at push time). The detail is a runtime\noptimization, not a correctness concern.\n\n### Output-push\n\n```c\nfor each outgoing connection j:\n    if output_edge_native[j]:\n        push native_bytes      to consumer->ring_native\n        push (NATIVE, idx)     to consumer->ring_order\n    else:\n        if output_was_native: json_bytes = spec->native_to_json(native_bytes)\n        push json_bytes        to consumer->ring_json\n        push (JSON, idx)       to consumer->ring_order\n```\n\nIf the spec produced native and multiple cross-language consumers\nexist, the dispatch calls `native_to_json` once and reuses the\nJSON bytes across every cross-language push (optimization, not\nrequired for correctness).\n\n## Edge cases\n\n- **All-same-language graph**: every connection's\n  `output_edge_native[j]` is true; `ring_json` stays unallocated\n  across every slot; activity is on `ring_native` and `ring_order`\n  only.\n- **All-different-language graph**: every connection routes to\n  `ring_json`; `ring_native` stays unallocated.\n- **Mixed fan-in**: both rings active on the same slot. Consumer\n  sees a mix of native and JSON cells, picked apart by\n  `ring_order`.\n- **Type changes mid-development**: a wire that was Lua→Lua\n  becomes Lua→C when the user changes a downstream box's language.\n  The graph loader recomputes the per-edge bits on the next load;\n  no data conversion at runtime, no migration.\n\n## Implementation cost\n\n- **Slot store**: two rings per slot instead of one, plus the\n  ordering ring. Allocate `ring_json` and `ring_order` lazily —\n  same-language-only slots pay nothing for them. Per-cell cost:\n  one extra ordering-ring write on push, one extra read on pop.\n- **Spec interface**: one signature change to the `invoke`\n  callback adding `input_native[]` and `output_native`. Every\n  spec's `invoke` gains a per-input branch. Lua's branch is\n  almost free — both decoders (`json_to_native` and the future\n  native one) already exist as bridges. C and Bash need their\n  JSON bridges written, which is shared with the broader\n  cross-language work in 307/308.\n- **Dispatch**: one extra step on read (consult ordering ring),\n  one extra branch on push (which ring to write).\n\nThe win is exactness: same-language values stay native even when\ntheir port has a cross-language sibling producer.\n\n## Future: whole-program same-language compilation (issue 313)\n\n313 describes merging all same-language source into one\ntranslation unit per language at compile time. The merge does\n**not** eliminate the slot store for same-language wires — boxes\nstill run as separate tasks in the thread pool, possibly on\ndifferent workers, so values still need a place to wait for\nsibling inputs to arrive. The thread pool's parallelism depends\non tasks being scheduled independently; collapsing same-language\nhops to direct function calls would serialize execution and defeat\nthe pool.\n\nWhat 313 thins is the **spec invocation overhead**: for boxes in\nthe merged region, the dispatch can call the function directly on\nthe worker's `lua_State` (or compiled C code) without going\nthrough dlsym + bytes-marshal + bridge. The dual-ring slot shape\nremains; 313 makes the native-ring's read/write cheaper. The two\nlayers stack, they don't cancel.\n\n## Resolved design choice: JSON, not type-annotated raw bytes\n\nAn earlier note asked whether cross-language wires should use\ntype-annotated raw byte encodings (length-prefixed ints, raw\nfloats, etc.) instead of JSON. **Decided: JSON across the board\nfor the cross-language ring.** Rationale:\n\n- JSON's self-describing structure makes a new language one\n  spec-pair of `native_to_json` / `json_to_native` — no per-pair\n  language paths.\n- The native ring's format is each spec's private choice, so the\n  per-language performance flexibility is there without universal\n  type-tag complexity.\n- Sentinel primitives in JSON (`$ref`, `$function_pointer`,\n  `$lang_opaque`) carry the structurally awkward cases without\n  forcing the wire format to grow new top-level shapes.\n\n## Suggested implementation sequence\n\n1. **Extend the slot data structure**. Add `ring_native`,\n   `ring_json`, `ring_order` to the slot type in\n   `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}`. Allocate `ring_json` and\n   `ring_order` lazily.\n2. **Extend the spec `invoke` signature**. Update `lang_invoke_fn`\n   in `langs/lang-spec.h` to add `const int *input_native`,\n   `int output_native`. Touch every spec's `invoke` stub to accept\n   (and initially ignore) the new parameters.\n3. **Update dispatch input-read** (`src/012-dispatch.c`). Pop\n   `ring_order` first, then the named ring. Populate\n   `input_native[]` per input port.\n4. **Update dispatch output-push** (`src/012-dispatch.c`). Select\n   the consumer's ring based on `output_edge_native[j]`. Write\n   the ordering-ring tuple. Reuse JSON across cross-language\n   consumers when the producer fanned to multiple.\n5. **Lua spec** (`langs/lua/spec.c`). Branch per `input_native[i]`\n   — decode native or JSON accordingly. Pick the native byte\n   format (start with `cjson.encode`; refine later).\n6. **C and Bash spec bridges**. Implement `native_to_json` and\n   `json_to_native` for C and Bash so cross-language pushes work.\n   C's native form is `memcpy` of the wrapper's declared struct;\n   Bash's native form is plain strings.\n7. **Remove `use_native_invoke`**. Drop the per-box field from\n   `box_t` and its computation from `graph_load`. The dispatch\n   consults the ordering ring's per-cell tag, not a per-box\n   summary.\n8. **Tests**. A 100k-iteration Lua→Lua pipeline uses `ring_native`\n   exclusively. A mixed-lang pipeline exercises both rings. A\n   mixed-fan-in port shows both rings active on one slot.\n\n## Relevant files\n\n- `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}` — slot data structure; the\n  dual-ring extension lands here.\n- `src/010-graph-loader.{c,h}` — per-edge classification (already\n  done at `010-graph-loader.c:689-754`).\n- `src/012-dispatch.{c,h}` — input-read, output-push.\n- `langs/lang-spec.h` — `invoke` signature extension.\n- `langs/lua/spec.c`, `langs/c/spec.c`, `langs/bash/spec.c` — per-spec\n  implementations.\n- `issues/302-wire-value-slot-store.md` — original single-ring\n  slot design (completed); the dual-ring extension is the structural\n  amendment captured here.\n- `issues/306-lua-language-spec.md`, `307-c-language-spec.md`,\n  `308-bash-language-spec.md` — per-spec typed marshalling lands on\n  top of this issue.\n- `issues/313-research-whole-program-same-language-merge.md` —\n  follow-on that thins spec invocation for merged regions.\n- `issues/245-wire-color-by-cross-language-classification.md` —\n  editor-side visual reflection of the per-edge classification\n  (green for JSON, gray for native).\n\n## Implementation status\n\nWhat's in place:\n\n- `lang_spec_t` carries `invoke_native`, `invoke_json`,\n  `native_to_json`, `json_to_native` slots (current shape — to be\n  collapsed to a single `invoke` with per-input/per-output flags\n  per this issue).\n- Lua's `native_to_json` and `json_to_native` are real\n  (`langs/lua/spec.c:335` and `:437`).\n- `input_edge_native[]` and `output_edge_native[]` are computed at\n  graph load (`src/010-graph-loader.c:689-754`).\n- Per-box `use_native_invoke` summary is computed and consulted by\n  dispatch — to be removed.\n- **Slice 1 complete (2026-05-21).** Dual-ring slot data structure\n  in `src/009-slot-store.{c,h}`: `SLOT_FLAG_DUAL_RING`,\n  `SLOT_RING_NATIVE` / `SLOT_RING_JSON` constants, three internal\n  rings per slot (native data, JSON data, ordering) under one\n  per-slot spinlock, new API `slot_push_native`, `slot_push_json`,\n  `slot_pop_ordered`. Single per-slot lock makes the data+ordering\n  write atomic so consumers see push pairs intact. On non-dual\n  slots `slot_push_native` and `slot_pop_ordered` alias to the\n  existing `slot_push` / `slot_pop`; `slot_push_json` returns -1.\n  On dual slots the old single-ring API returns -1 (caller must\n  pick a ring). `slot_has_value` and `slot_fill_count` consult\n  the ordering ring on dual slots so spawn-on-input-ready and\n  test introspection still work. Five new tests in\n  `tests/009-slot-store-test.c` cover the happy paths\n  (native-only, mixed arrival order, wraparound), the\n  order-ring-as-binding-capacity-constraint, and the back-compat\n  cases.\n\n- **Slice 2 complete (2026-05-21).** `lang_invoke_fn` typedef in\n  `langs/lang-spec.h` gained `const int *input_native` (between\n  `input_sizes` and `n_inputs`) and `int output_native` (after\n  `n_inputs`, before `out_buf`). Because all three callback slots\n  (`invoke`, `invoke_native`, `invoke_json`) share the same\n  typedef, the change propagates automatically. Every spec's\n  invoke function picked up the new params and currently ignores\n  them — the per-input branching is slice 4 (Lua) and 307 / 308\n  (C and Bash). Dispatch's `do_call_box` passes `NULL` /\n  `0` placeholder values; slice 3 wires up the real per-cell\n  flags from the dual-ring's ordering pop. Eleven direct\n  call sites in the spec test files updated to the new signature.\n- **Slice 3 complete (2026-05-21).** Call-box input ports become\n  dual-ring slots (in `graph_attach_runtime`) when their shape\n  permits — restrictions inherited from slice 1: not LARGE_VALUE,\n  not TAGGED. Dispatch's `read_inputs` consults\n  `slot_flags(slot_id)` and routes dual-ring slots through\n  `slot_pop_ordered`, populating `input_native[i]` from the\n  per-cell `which_ring` tag returned by the pop; single-ring\n  slots keep the existing `slot_peek` / `slot_pop` path and\n  derive `input_native[i]` from the per-port\n  `input_edge_native[i]` classification. Push side\n  (`push_one_connection`) now takes the producer's connection\n  index, branches on the consumer slot's flags, and picks\n  `slot_push_native` vs `slot_push_json` based on the producer's\n  `output_edge_native[j]`. Literal pushes from\n  `dispatch_push_literals` go through `slot_push_native` (literals\n  are configured in the consumer's own box JSON, so semantically\n  same-language). `do_write_box`'s `read_inputs` call passes\n  `NULL` for the flag array since write boxes aren't spec-invoked.\n  Added `slot_flags(s, id)` accessor to the slot store API so\n  dispatch can branch without storing per-port bookkeeping.\n  Existing tests stay green (22 slot-store, 14 graph-loader,\n  7 dispatch, 11 integration including multi-language `pipeline`\n  and `driver-test`); test fixtures that pushed directly via\n  `slot_push` on what's now a dual-ring slot were migrated to\n  `slot_push_native`.\n\n- **Slice 4 complete (2026-05-21).** Lua spec's `lua_invoke`\n  branches per `input_native[i]`: NATIVE (or NULL slice-2 callers)\n  push via `lua_pushlstring` exactly as before; JSON\n  (`input_native[i] == 0`) calls a new shared `parse_json_to_stack`\n  helper that lifts the bytes into a Lua value via the existing\n  `decode_node`. On JSON parse failure the spec falls back to\n  `lua_pushlstring` silently — that's the bridge to today's\n  cross-language producers (C / Bash) which haven't started\n  emitting real JSON yet; preserves backward compatibility while\n  the input-format contract tightens. `lua_json_to_native` was\n  refactored to use the same helper, just with `verbose=1` so the\n  bridge path remains loud on parse failures (a cross-language\n  wire that ought to carry JSON but doesn't is a real wire\n  error). Output-side branching on `output_native` deferred —\n  that's a coordinated change with the C and Bash specs and the\n  dispatch's per-call output format computation; see \"what's\n  still ahead\" below. Three tests in `tests/306-lua-spec-test.c`\n  cover the new behavior: `invoke_json_input` (JSON table parses\n  and indexes correctly), `invoke_native_input_with_flag`\n  (NATIVE flag keeps the raw-string path), and\n  `invoke_json_input_falls_back_to_raw` (non-JSON bytes with\n  JSON flag fall back gracefully).\n\n- **Slice 4.5 (2026-05-21) — per-wire writing style + Lua JSON\n  output.** Two coordinated halves:\n  - **Per-wire push.** Each wire keeps its own format. The\n    dispatch picks the consumer slot's native or JSON ring per\n    outgoing connection based on `output_edge_native[j]`. A box\n    with mixed fan-out (some same-lang consumers, some\n    cross-lang) lets each consumer get the right format for its\n    own wire.\n  - **Lua writes JSON when any consumer is cross-language.** The\n    spec passes `output_native = 1` to invoke if every outgoing\n    wire is same-language, else `0`. On the `0` branch the Lua\n    spec serializes the return via the same encoder that powers\n    the `native_to_json` bridge, so cross-language consumers\n    receive parseable JSON (numbers, strings, tables, arrays).\n    On `1` the spec keeps the existing tostring coercion. C-side\n    JSON-input acceptance landed in 307 alongside this, so a Lua\n    → C wire with a primitive payload now round-trips through\n    real JSON in both directions. The pipeline integration test\n    exercises this end-to-end: Lua produces a number, JSON-\n    encodes to bare digits, C unwraps and reads.\n  - **Known limitation for mixed fan-out.** A producer with\n    both same-lang and cross-lang consumers writes one format\n    (JSON, picked by the AND of `output_edge_native[j]`). The\n    same-lang consumers in that case receive JSON bytes through\n    their native ring — Lua's input-side parse-with-fallback\n    absorbs this, so primitives still round-trip. A full per-wire\n    output (producer leaves the value in its runtime; dispatch\n    calls `native_to_json` per cross-lang wire to materialize\n    JSON only where needed) is a deeper contract change tracked\n    as a future slice.\n- **Slice 5 complete (2026-05-21).** The per-box\n  `use_native_invoke` field and its computation are gone. The\n  dispatch's invoke-fn selector collapsed to a single\n  `spec->invoke` call — the per-input and per-output format flags\n  carry the information the old `invoke_native` / `invoke_json`\n  callback split was meant to encode. The spec interface's\n  `invoke_native` and `invoke_json` slots remain as optional\n  no-ops; future specs can specialize on them if profiling\n  justifies, but the dispatch no longer requires them. Three\n  test-side references in `tests/010-graph-loader-test.c` that\n  asserted on the per-box bit were updated to assert on the\n  per-edge bits they were already alongside.\n\nWhat still depends on this issue but lives elsewhere:\n\n- **307 / 308 — C and Bash JSON-input handling**, plus the\n  symmetric output side. Once those land, slice 4.5's deferred\n  Lua-side JSON write can also land and cross-language tables /\n  structured values will round-trip.\n- **318 — sentinel primitives**, the JSON-wire shapes that carry\n  closures / pointers / opaque language values across the\n  cross-language ring.\n\n## Open questions\n\n- **Lua's native byte format**: start with `cjson.encode` (still a\n  win over the json-ring path because the consumer's spec\n  recognises its own format and skips a parse), or jump straight\n  to msgpack? Defer the decision; the dual-ring shape doesn't\n  depend on the choice.\n- **Sharing JSON across multiple cross-language consumers**: the\n  dispatch can call `native_to_json` once and push the same bytes\n  to N cross-language consumer slots. Worthwhile when N > 1; the\n  push path needs a small buffer to hold the JSON across the fan\n  loop. Implement after the basic path works.\n- **Bash native = JSON?**: Bash has no native form distinct from\n  strings, and JSON is already a string. Bash's native ring may\n  hold JSON-shaped strings (effectively aliasing the JSON ring's\n  content). Resolve by inspection once the bash spec writeup\n  starts.\n",
```

```json
{
  "sessionId":"8ec29876-d560-4fbc-934b-227cde0b3a68",
  "timestamp":"2026-05-22T19:39:10.018Z",

  "message":{
    "content":[{
        "type":"tool_result",
        "content":"1\t# 318 — Sentinel primitives in the cross-language JSON wire\n2\t\n3\t## Status\n4\topen\n5\t\n6\t## Concept\n7\t\n8\tJSON's structure covers six kinds of value cleanly — object,\n9\tarray, number, string, bool, null — and the cross-language ring\n10\tin the dual-ring slot (issue 312) carries JSON for any wire that\n11\tcrosses a language boundary. The structural cases that don't fit\n12\tthose six kinds — Lua closures, libcurl handles, C++ class\n13\tinstances, file handles, function pointers, large byte buffers\n14\twhere serialization would be expensive — need somewhere to live\n15\tin the JSON ring without forcing the wire format to grow new\n16\ttop-level shapes.\n17\t\n18\t**Sentinel primitives** are JSON object shapes with reserved key\n19\tnames that mean \"this isn't a structural value, it's a reference\n20\tto something the consumer must reconstruct using language-specific\n21\tmachinery.\"\n22\t\n23\tThree kinds, each solving a different structural gap:\n24\t\n25\t```json\n26\t{\"$ref\":              {\"ptr\": \"0x...\",  \"len\": 1048576,\n27\t                       \"type\": \"bytes\"}}\n28\t{\"$function_pointer\": {\"addr\": \"0x...\", \"signature\": \"int(void*,void*)\"}}\n29\t{\"$lang_opaque\":      {\"lang\": \"lua\",   \"tag\": 12345,\n30\t                       \"shape\": \"coroutine\"}}\n31\t```\n32\t\n33\tSentinels are **emitted by the producer's spec** when its language\n34\thas a value JSON can't represent structurally. They are\n35\t**reconstructed by the consumer's spec** using whatever runtime\n36\tmachinery that language provides (LuaJIT FFI, ctypes for Python,\n37\tdirect cast for C, etc.). Wires whose consumer cannot reconstruct\n38\ta sentinel kind **fail at compile time** — the editor catches the\n39\tmismatch when the user wires a function-pointer output into a\n40\tBash input.\n41\t\n42\t## Why these three, not more\n43\t\n44\tThe three sentinels carve up the structural gap by ownership:\n45\t\n46\t- **`$ref`** — bytes in a shared heap. The producer wrote the\n47\t  bytes to the large-value heap (issue 302 / `src/015-large-value-heap.c`)\n48\t  and emits a pointer-and-length reference. The bytes are\n49\t  immutable for the wire's lifetime; refcount tracks consumers.\n50\t  Solves: \"I have a megabyte to share; copying it through the\n51\t  slot would be wasteful.\"\n52\t- **`$function_pointer`** — a callable, identified by a raw\n53\t  address and a signature interpretable in C-function-pointer\n54\t  syntax. The address is process-local. The signature is the\n55\t  contract between producer and consumer. Solves: \"I have a\n56\t  function I want my consumer to be able to call directly.\"\n57\t- **`$lang_opaque`** — a language-private value. The producer\n58\t  tags it with the language name and a worker-private reference\n59\t  the producer's spec can resolve back. Solves: \"I have a value\n60\t  with no general structural representation; only my own\n61\t  language knows what it is.\"\n62\t\n63\tAnything that doesn't fit one of the three either (a) has a\n64\tJSON-structural representation and shouldn't be a sentinel, or\n65\t(b) is genuinely unrepresentable and the wire is wrong, not the\n66\tformat.\n67\t\n68\t## Producer protocol\n69\t\n70\tEach language spec's `native_to_json` walks its native values and\n71\temits the appropriate sentinel when it encounters a non-structural\n72\tvalue. The spec author writes the emit logic; it's not generic.\n73\t\n74\t### Lua\n75\t\n76\t`encode_value` in `langs/lua/spec.c:188` currently hard-errors on\n77\tfunction / userdata / thread / lightuserdata. The extension:\n78\t\n79\t- **Function** (`LUA_TFUNCTION`) — if the function is a C function\n80\t  registered with FFI, emit `$function_pointer` with its FFI\n81\t  signature; if it's a Lua function, emit `$lang_opaque` with\n82\t  `lang: \"lua\"` and a tag the spec can use to resolve the\n83\t  function back later (a registry index, for example).\n84\t- **Userdata** with an FFI ctype — emit `$function_pointer` if\n85\t  the type is a function pointer; `$ref` if the type is a sized\n86\t  buffer; `$lang_opaque` otherwise.\n87\t- **Coroutine** (`LUA_TTHREAD`) — `$lang_opaque` with\n88\t  `shape: \"coroutine\"`.\n89\t- **Large strings or tables** — at the spec's discretion, the\n90\t  producer may write the value to the large-value heap and emit\n91\t  `$ref` instead of inlining the bytes. Decision is per-call,\n92\t  driven by size threshold or output annotation.\n93\t\n94\t### C\n95\t\n96\tThe C spec's generated wrapper (issue 307) knows the box's\n97\tdeclared output type. The wrapper emits the right sentinel\n98\tshape based on the type:\n99\t\n100\t- A declared `function_pointer` return — `$function_pointer`\n101\t  with the type's signature.\n102\t- A declared `bytes` return whose size exceeds the inline\n103\t  threshold — `$ref` with the bytes written to the heap.\n104\t- A declared opaque struct — `$lang_opaque` if the struct's\n105\t  shape is C-only.\n106\t\n107\t### Bash\n108\t\n109\tBash has no native values besides strings; sentinels don't apply\n110\ton the emit side. A Bash producer always emits structural JSON\n111\tstrings.\n112\t\n113\t## Consumer protocol\n114\t\n115\tEach spec's `json_to_native` walks the JSON tree and, when it\n116\tsees a sentinel key, reconstructs the value using its language's\n117\tmachinery. The reconstruction is the spec author's responsibility.\n118\t\n119\t### Lua\n120\t\n121\t```c\n122\t// inside lua_json_to_native, on seeing {\"$function_pointer\": {...}}:\n123\tconst char *sig = json_string_value(obj, \"signature\");\n124\tuintptr_t   addr = json_int_value(obj, \"addr\");\n125\tlua_getglobal(L, \"ffi\");\n126\tlua_getfield(L, -1, \"cast\");\n127\tlua_pushstring(L, sig);                 // \"int (*)(void*, void*)\"\n128\tlua_pushinteger(L, (lua_Integer)addr);\n129\tlua_call(L, 2, 1);\n130\t// stack top: an FFI callable Lua can invoke as compare_fn(a, b)\n131\t```\n132\t\n133\t`$ref` reconstruction in Lua copies the bytes (`lua_pushlstring(ptr, len)`)\n134\tsince Lua's value model needs values in Lua's heap. The\n135\tproducer's refcount handles the lifetime.\n136\t\n137\t`$lang_opaque` with `lang: \"lua\"` resolves the tag through the\n138\tworker's per-worker registry; with `lang != \"lua\"` it's a wire\n139\tcompile error.\n140\t\n141\t### C\n142\t\n143\tDirect casts. `$function_pointer` becomes a typed function\n144\tpointer the wrapper can invoke. `$ref` is a `(void*, size_t)`\n145\tpair the wrapper hands to the user's function. `$lang_opaque`\n146\twith `lang: \"c\"` is a typed-struct pointer; other langs fail.\n147\t\n148\t### Bash\n149\t\n150\tBash cannot reconstruct any sentinel except possibly `$ref`\n151\t(by reading the bytes from the file the marshaller staged at the\n152\tprocess boundary). `$function_pointer` and `$lang_opaque` fail\n153\tunless the producer chose the cross-process escape hatch (see\n154\tbelow).\n155\t\n156\t## Compile-time validation\n157\t\n158\tThe compile step walks every wire and checks:\n159\t\n160\t- If the producer's declared output type can emit sentinel kind\n161\t  K (per the producer language's spec capability), then\n162\t- The consumer's language must be able to reconstruct sentinel\n163\t  kind K (per the consumer language's spec capability).\n164\t- If not, **the compile fails** with the specific wire and the\n165\t  specific sentinel kind named.\n166\t\n167\tThis is editor-time feedback. The user fixes the wire\n168\t(reroute it, change the consumer's language, attach a custom\n169\ttranslation per issue 246) before the program ever runs.\n170\t\n171\tThe per-spec capability is a static fact each spec declares.\n172\tAdding a new language K declares K's emit set and reconstruct\n173\tset; the compile validator consults the declaration.\n174\t\n175\t## Cross-process escape hatch for `$function_pointer`\n176\t\n177\tBash (and any other process-isolated language) can't dereference\n178\tan in-process function-pointer address. Two options when a\n179\tfunction pointer's wire targets such a consumer:\n180\t\n181\t1. **Fail at compile time** (the default). Clear, honest.\n182\t2. **Producer-side compiled binary** (opt-in). When the C spec\n183\t   detects that a function-pointer output is wired to a\n184\t   cross-process consumer, it compiles the function into a\n185\t   standalone executable in `compiled/cross-process/<box>__<port>.bin`.\n186\t   The sentinel becomes:\n187\t\n188\t   ```json\n189\t   {\"$function_pointer\": {\"binary\": \"compiled/cross-process/...\",\n190\t                          \"signature\": \"int(int,int)\"}}\n191\t   ```\n192\t\n193\t   Bash invokes the binary with `$(./bin args...)` per call. Cost:\n194\t   process spawn per invocation (1-10 ms). The escape hatch is\n195\t   opt-in per box output — the user explicitly chooses the cost\n196\t   in the inspector.\n197\t\n198\tThe default behaviour is option 1 because hiding the spawn cost\n199\twould mislead the user about runtime behaviour. The user picks\n200\ttheir language; the cost follows from the choice. Option 2 is\n201\tthe escape hatch for cases where the user genuinely needs\n202\tC-from-Bash and accepts the cost.\n203\t\n204\t## Relationship to custom translation specs (issue 246)\n205\t\n206\tSentinels are the **spec author's tool** for representing\n207\tnon-structural values. Custom translations are the **user's\n208\ttool** for interpreting them (or any other value) when the\n209\tdefault reconstruction isn't what they want.\n210\t\n211\tThe escalation path:\n212\t\n213\t1. Default spec encode / decode handles JSON-structural values\n214\t   transparently.\n215\t2. When the producer's value can't be encoded structurally, the\n216\t   spec author's emit logic produces the right sentinel.\n217\t3. When the consumer's spec can reconstruct the sentinel\n218\t   generically (FFI cast, ctypes, direct pointer), the default\n219\t   reconstruction runs.\n220\t4. When the user wants a different interpretation than the\n221\t   default (treat a function pointer as a method on a class\n222\t   instance, dereference a `$ref` and parse its bytes as a\n223\t   custom format, etc.), they attach a per-port custom\n224\t   translation that overrides the default reconstruction.\n225\t\n226\tSo sentinels and custom translations are layered: sentinels\n227\tprovide the *bytes that arrive at the wire* for non-structural\n228\tvalues; custom translations decide *what to do with those bytes*\n229\twhen the default isn't enough.\n230\t\n231\t## Where in the dispatch this lives\n232\t\n233\tSentinels live inside the JSON ring of the dual-ring slot. The\n234\tslot store (issue 312) doesn't know about them — it just carries\n235\tbytes. The producer's `native_to_json` emits them when writing\n236\tto the JSON ring; the consumer's `json_to_native` recognizes\n237\tand reconstructs them when reading from the JSON ring.\n238\t\n239\tInside the native ring, sentinels don't appear — native values\n240\tare encoded in the language's own binary form, which can\n241\trepresent its own callables / opaque values directly without\n242\tneeding a JSON-side hack.\n243\t\n244\t## Relevant files\n245\t\n246\t- `langs/lang-spec.h` — bridges (`native_to_json`,\n247\t  `json_to_native`) are where sentinel handling lives per spec.\n248\t- `langs/lua/spec.c:188` (`encode_value`) — emit-side extension\n249\t  point. Currently hard-errors on function / userdata / thread;\n250\t  this issue replaces those errors with sentinel emission.\n251\t- `langs/lua/spec.c:370` (`decode_node`) — reconstruct-side\n252\t  extension point. Add sentinel recognition before generic\n253\t  object handling.\n254\t- `langs/c/spec.c` — C spec's `native_to_json` / `json_to_native`\n255\t  (currently NULL); both grow when this issue lands.\n256\t- `langs/bash/spec.c` — Bash spec; only `$ref` is reconstructable.\n257\t- `src/015-large-value-heap.c` — `$ref` storage.\n258\t- `scripts/soramech-compile.sh` — compile-time wire validation\n259\t  walks producer/consumer sentinel capabilities.\n260\t- `issues/312-same-language-wire-fast-path.md` — dual-ring shape;\n261\t  sentinels ride inside its JSON ring.\n262\t- `issues/246-custom-translation-specs-per-port.md` — user-side\n263\t  override layered on top of sentinel reconstruction.\n264\t- `docs/001-architecture.md` — wire format section; gains a brief\n265\t  sentinel reference.\n266\t\n267\t## Suggested implementation sequence\n268\t\n269\t1. **Define the canonical JSON shapes**. Three sentinel kinds,\n270\t   exact key names, value-object schemas. Document in\n271\t   `docs/001-architecture.md`.\n272\t2. **Lua emit**: replace the hard-errors in `encode_value` with\n273\t   sentinel emission per the producer protocol above. Start with\n274\t   `$function_pointer` for FFI callables and `$lang_opaque` for\n275\t   Lua functions / coroutines.\n276\t3. **Lua reconstruct**: extend `decode_node` to recognize the\n277\t   three sentinels and reconstruct them.\n278\t4. **Per-spec capability declarations**. Each spec declares its\n279\t   emit set (sentinel kinds it can produce) and reconstruct set\n280\t   (sentinel kinds it can consume). Lives next to the\n281\t   `lang_spec_t` declaration.\n282\t5. **C spec sentinels**. Emit via the generated wrapper based on\n283\t   declared output types; reconstruct via direct cast.\n284\t6. **Bash spec sentinels**. Only `$ref` on the reconstruct side\n285\t   (read bytes from the marshaller's staged file).\n286\t7. **Compile-time validation**. Walk every wire; check the\n287\t   producer's emit set against the consumer's reconstruct set;\n288\t   fail with a clear message on mismatch.\n289\t8. **Cross-process escape hatch for `$function_pointer`**.\n290\t   Opt-in flag in the box output declaration; the C spec emits a\n291\t   standalone binary instead of (or alongside) the in-process\n292\t   address.\n293\t\n294\t## Open questions\n295\t\n296\t- **`$ref` lifetime across iterator-fed slots**. A producer's\n297\t  output is referenced by an N-cell ring of consumer slots; each\n298\t  cell holds the `$ref`, each pop decrements. Already supported\n299\t  by the large-value heap's refcount, but worth verifying the\n300\t  test coverage when sentinels land.\n301\t- **Sentinel inside sentinel.** A `$ref` whose bytes are\n302\t  themselves JSON containing a `$function_pointer` is legal but\n303\t  weird. Document the recursive case; probably falls out of the\n304\t  walker for free.\n305\t- **Versioning the sentinel format**. The reserved key names\n306\t  (`$ref`, `$function_pointer`, `$lang_opaque`) and the\n307\t  value-object schemas are part of the compile-time contract.\n308\t  Changing them is breaking. Worth a one-line stability note\n309\t  somewhere.\n310\t- **Cross-process `$ref` semantics**. In-process `$ref` is a raw\n311\t  pointer + length. Cross-process (Bash via UDS), the marshaller\n312\t  stages the bytes at the socket boundary. Decide whether the\n313\t  Bash-side `$ref` reconstruction reads from a file path or\n314\t  inlines the bytes in the request. The latter is simpler; the\n315\t  former scales to bigger values.\n316\t- **`bytes` returns on the C spec currently passthrough raw bytes\n317\t  into the JSON ring.** The C spec's typed-output-JSON path\n318\t  (issue 307 item 2) wraps strings in JSON quotes and passes\n319\t  ints / doubles / bools through as valid JSON fragments — but for\n320\t  a declared `bytes` return it writes the raw payload verbatim,\n321\t  which is *not* valid JSON in the general case (non-UTF-8 bytes\n322\t  break parsers; embedded quotes / backslashes mangle the wire).\n323\t  The proper resolution belongs here, not in 307: small `bytes`\n324\t  outputs should be base64-wrapped into a JSON string (or its own\n325\t  sentinel kind), and large `bytes` outputs should follow the\n326\t  `$ref` path already described for the heap. Until that lands,\n327\t  cross-language `bytes` wires from C producers are an honest\n328\t  hazard — flag in the compile-time validator if possible, or at\n329\t  minimum document the gap so users don't wire a `bytes`\n330\t  C-output into a Lua / Bash consumer expecting clean JSON.\n331\t",

  "uuid":"042f9222-cf25-4a5c-8c75-bdd780d385d8",
```
